# Research Topic
Accelerating Graph Attention Network training

# Idea Info
{
    "Open Problems": "Current scalable training pipelines for Graph Attention Networks (GATs) still suffer from one of two bottlenecks: 1) node–wise, attention–aware samplers based on bandits (e.g. GNN-BS) provide optimal variance but do not correlate the sampling decisions of different seeds, so the same neighbour is fetched many times and the neighbourhood-explosion problem remains; 2) layer–wise samplers that correlate vertices (e.g. LABOR / PLADIES) or sub-graph samplers (GraphSAINT) are memory- and time-efficient, but are attention-agnostic – they cannot exploit the fact that only a small fraction of the neighbours finally receives high attention weights. No existing work unifies (i) layer–wise correlation, (ii) on-line, attention-driven importance sampling, and (iii) provable variance control. Bridging this gap would immediately cut both memory traffic and FLOPs for deep GAT/GATv2/GeniePath models and enable much larger batch sizes.",
    "Methods": "We propose HABLS (Hi-erarchical Adaptive Bandit Layer Sampling). 1) Start from LABOR’s idea: for a mini-batch of seed nodes S, compute a per-layer Poisson sampling probability q¹…qᴸ that guarantees Var(ĥ)=Var(NS,k). 2) Instead of materialising all q’s, we draw a small correlated candidate set C_ℓ using LABOR-0 (one pass) – typically 2–3×k unique vertices. 3) Inside each layer ℓ we run a light-weight adversarial bandit with multiple plays (EXP3.M): each action is a k-subset of C_ℓ. The reward of neighbour j at layer ℓ is r_j = −|∂L/∂H_ℓ,j|² (gradient magnitude) × α̂_ij   where α̂_ij is the current (unnormalised) attention score coming from the forward pass. 4) The bandit updates a set of weights w_ℓ and produces the final sample S_ℓ via DepRound so that  E[#samples]=k and the per-vertex inclusion probability p_ℓ(j)≈min(1,w_ℓ(j)). 5) Because C_ℓ is correlated across seeds, memory is kept small; because rewards use attention, useless vertices quickly receive low probability; and because inclusion probabilities are tracked, an unbiased estimator with variance ≤ 3×Var(NS) is obtained (proof mirrors GNN-BS.M + LABOR). 6) To accelerate back-prop, we apply Randomised Sparse Computation (RSC) only on layers whose running variance has already stabilised (measured by moving average of ‖g‖₂). 7) All components are differentiable / torch-autograd friendly and can be plugged into existing PyG or DGL GAT implementations by replacing their neighbour sampler.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Datasets: Reddit, Flickr, ogbn-products (large, shallow); ogbn-arxiv (medium, deeper); Cora & Pubmed (small, sanity-check).\nModels: GAT-2-layer (hidden 256), GATv2-4-layer (hidden 128), GeniePath-3-layer.\nBaselines: (a) NeighborSampling (NS) with fanout 25; (b) GraphSAINT-rw; (c) LABOR-*; (d) GNN-BS.M; (e) LABOR + RSC (no bandit).\nMetrics: test accuracy / micro-F1; wall-clock training time to reach 95% of best val acc; peak GPU memory; average #sampled vertices / edges per iteration; variance of estimator.\nProtocol: fixed seeds, Adam (lr 0.005), early stopping 50 epochs. For fairness, all methods use identical forward kernels; only the sampler and (for us) optional RSC differ.\nRobustness: repeat ×5 seeds; ablation w/o attention reward, w/o Poisson replacement, and with different k (10,25,50).",
    "Experimental Code": "import torch, torch.nn.functional as F\nfrom torch_geometric.loader import NeighborLoader\nfrom labor_sampler import labor_sample_once   # light-weight LABOR-0 (python re-impl)\nfrom dep_round import dep_round              # k-subset without replacement\n\nclass BanditLayerSampler:\n    def __init__(self,k,eta=0.3):\n        self.k=k; self.eta=eta; self.w=None\n    def sample(self,candidates,grad,att):\n        # candidates: tensor [m],  grad & att same length, detached\n        if self.w is None:\n            self.w=torch.ones_like(grad)\n        reward=(-grad.abs()*att).cpu()\n        p=(1-self.k*1e-3)*self.w/self.w.sum()+1e-3  # mix-in\n        subset_idx=dep_round(p,self.k)              # indices into candidates\n        # EXP3.M update\n        est=np.zeros_like(p); est[subset_idx]=reward[subset_idx]/p[subset_idx]\n        self.w*=torch.exp(self.eta*torch.tensor(est))\n        return candidates[subset_idx]\n\nclass HABLSampler:\n    def __init__(self,g,k_per_layer):\n        self.g=g; self.k=k_per_layer; self.layers=[BanditLayerSampler(k) for k in k_per_layer]\n    def __call__(self,seed_nodes,model):\n        neigh_dict={0:seed_nodes}\n        for l,bandit in enumerate(self.layers):\n            cand=labor_sample_once(self.g,neigh_dict[l],self.k[l]*3)   # candidate pool\n            # dummy forward to get attention & grad magnitudes\n            att=model.cached_att[l][cand]   # assume model stores raw α\n            grad=model.cached_grad[l][cand]\n            neigh_dict[l+1]=bandit.sample(cand,grad,att)\n        return [neigh_dict[l] for l in range(1,len(self.k)+1)]\n\n# attach to PyG GAT training loop\nsampler=HABLSampler(data.edge_index,[25,25])\nfor epoch in range(epochs):\n    for seeds in train_idx.split(batch_size):\n        sub_nodes_per_layer=sampler(seeds,model)\n        out=model.forward_with_fixed_neigh(seeds,sub_nodes_per_layer)\n        loss=F.nll_loss(out,y[seeds]); loss.backward(); opt.step()",
    "Expected Result": "HABLS reduces the average sampled vertices by ≈4× compared to NS and 1.7× compared to LABOR-*, while matching or improving test accuracy (↑0.5-1.0%). Training time drops by 30-50 % versus LABOR-* and >2× versus GraphSAINT. Estimator variance curves overlap with GNN-BS.M (≈2.5× Var(NS) bound). Peak GPU memory shrinks by ~35 %. Ablations show that removing attention-based rewards deteriorates accuracy by ~1.5 %, proving their benefit.",
    "Expected Conclusion": "HABLS demonstrates that combining layer-correlated Poisson sampling with on-line attention-aware bandit re-weighting yields a sampler that is simultaneously memory- and variance-efficient. It outperforms the state-of-the-art in both convergence speed and resource footprint without architectural changes to the GAT encoder. The framework is generic (works with GATv2, GeniePath, Transformer-like Graph models) and Python-only. Future work can extend HABLS with learned priors (edge features, historical gradients) and integrate it with distributed GPU training for trillion-edge graphs, bringing efficient attention-based GNNs closer to production scale."
}

# Novelty
HABLS unifies three lines of prior work that have so far been mutually exclusive. 1) It starts from LABOR’s layer–correlated Poisson sampling but keeps the per-node inclusion-probability bookkeeping so that the unbiased-variance guarantee is preserved. 2) It grafts on-line, attention-driven importance sampling via a multi-play bandit (EXP3.M) reminiscent of GNN-BS, yet does so at the layer level and on a small candidate set shared by all seeds – something explicitly left as future work in the bandit-sampler paper. 3) It couples the two with a deterministic DepRound k-subset generator so that the effective variance is bounded by 3×Var(NS), matching the theoretical optimum of GNN-BS while eliminating the redundancy of node-wise sampling. No related work (LABOR/PLADIES, GraphSAINT, GNN-BS, ANS-GT) performs attention-aware adaptive sampling with layer-wise correlation and provable variance. The additional integration with variance-conditioned RSC for backward sparsification is also absent from existing samplers. Hence the method introduces a sampling framework that is qualitatively new rather than a parameter tweak of existing ones.

# Significance
If the claimed 4× vertex reduction and 30-50 % wall-clock speed-up over the strongest layer samplers are borne out, HABLS would immediately lower memory bandwidth and FLOP costs for GAT/GATv2/GeniePath training on large graphs – a pain point that currently blocks industry-scale deployment of attentive GNNs. Because the algorithm is variance-controlled and implemented as a drop-in PyG/DGL sampler, it is likely to be adopted by practitioners. Academically, it answers two open questions stated in earlier papers: extending bandit samplers to layer-wise sampling and making LABOR attention-aware. Its ideas could transfer to Transformer-style graph models and distributed training. The impact is nevertheless confined to the training phase of a specific class of GNNs and does not alter predictive power; hence transformative but not field-redefining.

    "Title": "Bandit Samplers for Training Graph Neural Networks",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the intractability of computing optimal sampling distributions for variance-reduced samplers in Graph Neural Networks (GNNs), especially for general GNNs like Graph Attention Networks (GAT) where embeddings and learned weights change during training. It reformulates the optimization of sampling variance as an adversary bandit problem. The key contributions include: (1) Introducing a novel perspective to GNN samplers as a bandit problem, applicable to both GCNs and attentive GNNs, unlike existing methods. (2) Theoretically proving that the proposed algorithms' estimators asymptotically approach the optimal variance within a factor of 3. (3) Empirically demonstrating superior performance in terms of convergence and sample variance compared to state-of-the-art approaches on multiple public datasets.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves formulating the sampling variance optimization as an adversary bandit problem. Instead of directly computing intractable optimal sampling distributions, the approach maintains nonparametric estimates of the sampler. These estimates are iteratively updated towards optimal variance as partial knowledge about sampled neighbors is acquired. The reward for each action (choosing a set of neighbors) is defined as the negative derivative of the sampling variance. Two bandit algorithms are proposed: (1) GNN-BS, based on the adversary Multi-Armed Bandit (MAB) setting, where a single neighbor is chosen k times and the sampler is updated by EXP3. (2) GNN-BS.M, based on MAB with multiple plays, which uses an efficient k-combination sampler (DepRound) to select a k-element subset of neighbors once, and updates the sampler using EXP3.M. For attentive GNNs, adjusted feedback attention values are used to approximate true attention values given only sampled neighborhoods.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on five benchmark datasets: Cora, Pubmed, PPI, Reddit, and Flickr, using standard data splits. The GNN architectures evaluated included GCN, GAT, and GeniePath (GP), with a fixed two-layer structure. Hidden embedding dimensions were set to 16 for Cora and Pubmed, and 256 for PPI, Reddit, and Flickr. The number of multi-heads for attentive GNNs was set to 1. Comparison algorithms included GraphSAGE, FastGCN, LADIES, AS-GCN, S-GCN, ClusterGCN, GraphSAINT, and their attentive GNN variants (AS-GAT, GraphSAINT-GAT). Hyperparameters such as learning rate, L2-norm regularizers, and dropout rate were optimized via grid search. Sample sizes (k) for node-wise samplers varied by dataset (e.g., 1 for Cora/Pubmed, 10 for PPI/Reddit), while layer-wise samplers used specific layer-dependent sample sizes. Batch size was 256 for layer sampling approaches and S-GCN. Models were saved based on the best validation results, and testing results were reported. Micro F1 scores, convergence rates (epochs and time), and sample variance were used as evaluation metrics. Experiments were run 3 times (10 times for OGB protein dataset) to report mean and standard deviation.",

    "Title": "Are GATs Out of Balance?",
    "Main Contributions": "This research addresses the challenge of trainability and performance degradation in deeper Graph Attention Networks (GATs) with standard initialization. The main contributions include deriving a conservation law for GAT gradient flow dynamics, which explains why a significant portion of parameters struggle to change during training, especially in deeper networks. To mitigate this, the study proposes a balanced initialization scheme that enables more effective gradient propagation, allowing for the successful training of deeper GATs. This approach also achieves considerable speedup in training and convergence time compared to standard initialization, empirically establishing a causal link between trainability and parameter balancedness. The derived theorem also serves as a foundational step for studying the learning dynamics of positive homogeneous models with attention mechanisms.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves deriving a conservation law for GAT gradient flow dynamics. This derivation is based on the concept of rescale invariance, adapted from traditional deep neural networks, and applies to GATs with positive homogeneous activation functions (e.g., ReLU, LeakyReLU). The conservation law reveals a structural relationship between parameters and their gradients. Based on this theoretical insight, a balanced initialization scheme is devised. This scheme involves setting attention parameters to zero and scaling feature transformation weights layer-wise to ensure that the l2-norms of incoming and outgoing weights satisfy a specific balancedness condition (∥Wl[i, :]∥² - ∥al[i]∥² = ∥Wl+1[:, i]∥²). Additionally, a Looks-Linear-Orthogonal (LLortho) structure is used for initializing feature weights before balancing, which aims to achieve dynamical isometry and further enhance trainability, especially for deeper models.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted to validate the theoretical insights and demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed balanced initialization. Nine common benchmark datasets for semi-supervised node classification were used: Cora, Citeseer, Pubmed, Actor, Chameleon, Cornell, Squirrel, Texas, and Wisconsin. GAT models of varying depths (from 2 to 40 layers, with stress tests up to 64 and 80 layers) and a default width of 64 (with some tests at 512) were trained. Four initialization schemes were compared: Xavier (standard), Xavier with Zero Attention (ablation), Balanced Xavier, and Balanced LL-Orthogonal. Training was performed using Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD) and Adam optimizers for up to 5000 epochs, with learning rates adjusted for network depth. Performance was measured by mean test accuracy (%) ± 95% confidence interval over five runs and epochs to achieve the best model (training speedup). Visualizations of relative parameter change and relative gradient norms were also used. The experiments were run on Nvidia T4 Tensor Core GPUs or Nvidia GeForce RTX 3060 Laptop GPUs. Architectural variations like multiple attention heads, ELU activation, absence of weight sharing, dropout, and weight decay were also investigated, and comparisons were made against Lipschitz Normalization and other orthogonal initializations. The applicability to GCNs and ωGAT was also demonstrated.",
    "Limitations": "The derived conservation law is specific to the self-attention mechanisms found in the original GAT and GATv2 models, and their variations like ωGAT. It does not directly apply to other types of self-attention, such as dot-product self-attention found in models like SuperGAT or Transformers, which would require modifications to the conservation law. The theoretical assumption of positive homogeneous activation functions (e.g., ReLU) means that using non-homogeneous functions like ELU can negatively impact the orthogonality and balancedness properties of certain initializations (e.g., BalO). The study primarily focused on vanilla GATs without skip connections to isolate the contribution of the initialization scheme, although residual skip connections are mentioned as compatible if their parameters are initialized to zero.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include extending the study of learning dynamics to other positive homogeneous models that incorporate attention mechanisms, such as various Transformer architectures (e.g., vision transformers, which heavily rely on depth, and NLP transformers), and graph Transformers. Another promising area is to investigate how dynamical isometry can be achieved or effectively approximated in general Graph Neural Networks. Furthermore, deriving specific modifications to the conservation law for other attention-based models that utilize dot-product self-attention (e.g., SuperGAT) is identified as an intriguing direction.",
    "Experiment Code": "# From layers.py (GraphAttentionLayer initialization):\nself.W = nn.Parameter(torch.empty(size=(in_features, out_features)))\nnn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.W.data, gain=1.414)\nself.a = nn.Parameter(torch.empty(size=(2*out_features, 1)))\nnn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.a.data, gain=1.414)\nself.leakyrelu = nn.LeakyReLU(self.alpha)\n# Activation for concatenated layers:\n# F.elu(h_prime)\n\n# From layers.py (SpGraphAttentionLayer initialization):\nself.W = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(size=(in_features, out_features)))\nnn.init.xavier_normal_(self.W.data, gain=1.414)\nself.a = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(size=(1, 2*out_features)))\nnn.init.xavier_normal_(self.a.data, gain=1.414)\nself.leakyrelu = nn.LeakyReLU(self.alpha)\n# Activation for concatenated layers:\n# F.elu(h_prime)",
    "Experiment Result": "The core methodology describes a balanced initialization scheme, including setting attention parameters to zero, scaling feature transformation weights layer-wise based on l2-norms, and using a Looks-Linear-Orthogonal (LLortho) structure.     "Title": "GATE: How to Keep Out Intrusive Neighbors",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper identifies and experimentally demonstrates a structural limitation of Graph Attention Networks (GATs): their inability to switch off task-irrelevant neighborhood aggregation, which leads to over-smoothing and performance degradation, especially in deeper models and on heterophilic datasets. To address this, the authors propose GATE, an extension of GAT that can selectively switch neighborhood aggregation on or off as needed. GATE alleviates over-smoothing, benefits from higher depth for non-linear feature transformations, often outperforms GATs on real-world heterophilic datasets by down-weighting unrelated neighbors, and offers interpretable self-attention coefficients. GATE achieves a new state-of-the-art test accuracy on the OGB-arxiv dataset (79.57 ± 0.84%) and introduces a synthetic test bed for analyzing adaptive neighborhood aggregation schemes.",
    "Methodology": "The methodology builds on theoretical insights derived from a conservation law of GAT gradient flow dynamics, explaining why GATs struggle to switch off aggregation due to norm constraints on attention parameters. GATE modifies the GAT architecture by introducing separate attention parameters, `as` and `at`, for the node's own features and neighborhood features, respectively. This modification allows GATE to flexibly weigh the importance of node features versus neighborhood features. The attention coefficients `euv` in GAT (Eq. 3) are modified in GATE (Eq. 4) to `euv = (1u̸=vas + 1u=vat)⊤ · ϕ(Ulhl−1 u + Vlhl−1 v)`, where `Ul` and `Vl` are independent matrices, or shared (`Wl = Ul = Vl`) in GATE S. This design enables GATE to switch off neighborhood aggregation in a well-trainable parameter regime, unlike GAT. The activation function `ϕ` in GATE is ReLU to allow interpretation of parameter signs.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experimental setup includes a synthetic test bed for node classification, comprising two problem types: self-sufficient learning (label-relevant information only in node's own features) and neighbor-dependent learning (label-relevant information in k-hop neighbors' features). These were constructed using Erdős–Rényi (ER) graphs and the Cora dataset structure with original or randomized labels and one-hot encoded node features. Real-world evaluations were conducted on five heterophilic benchmark datasets (roman-empire, amazon-ratings, questions, minesweeper, tolokers) and three OGB datasets (OGB-arxiv, OGB-products, OGB-mag), as well as five smaller datasets with varying homophily levels (Cora, Citeseer, Actor, Texas, Wisconsin). Performance was measured by test accuracy or AUC-ROC. Models were trained using the Adam optimizer for a maximum of 10000, 2000, or 5000 epochs, without weight decay or dropout regularization to isolate architectural effects. Baselines included GAT, MLP, MLP+GAT (alternating MLP and GAT layers), FAGCN, GraphSAGE, and ωGAT. Over-smoothing was quantitatively analyzed using the GAT energy (EGAT) measure.",
    "Limitations": "The paper highlights limitations of GATs, which GATE aims to resolve, rather than inherent limitations of GATE itself. For GATE, achieving 100% test accuracy on the synthetic neighbor-dependent learning problem was challenging, attributed to data points near a not-crisply defined decision boundary. For smaller real-world datasets, shallower models performed best, suggesting deeper GATE models without skip connections or regularization might be prone to overfitting. The paper also notes that the notion of 'over-smoothing' is task-dependent, and determining the optimal degree of smoothness or the threshold for 'over'-smoothing is complex, meriting further analysis.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The authors suggest that GATE is a suitable candidate to answer highly debated questions related to the importance of a given graph structure for standard tasks. They also propose combining GATE's capabilities with other architectures, such as leveraging FAGCN's capacity to learn negative associations with neighboring nodes. Furthermore, they suggest that it would be interesting to derive conservation laws inherent to other architectures like FAGCN and GraphSAGE to understand how they govern parameter behavior.",

    "Title": "GraphSAINT: Graph Sampling Based Inductive Learning Method",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces GraphSAINT (Graph SAmpling based INductive learning meThod), a novel approach to address the scalability challenges, particularly the 'neighbor explosion' problem, in training Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs) on large graphs and with deep layers. Unlike prior layer-sampling methods, GraphSAINT constructs minibatches by first sampling a subgraph from the training graph and then building a complete GCN on this small, well-connected subgraph. Key contributions include normalization techniques to eliminate bias introduced by graph sampling, and sampling algorithms designed for variance reduction. GraphSAINT decouples sampling from propagation, allowing integration with various GCN architectures like graph attention and jumping connections. It demonstrates superior performance in both accuracy and training time, achieving new state-of-the-art F1 scores of 0.995 for PPI and 0.970 for Reddit.",
    "Methodology": "GraphSAINT's core methodology involves constructing minibatches by sampling the training graph itself, rather than individual nodes or edges across GCN layers. The process begins with light-weight pre-processing to estimate the probability of nodes and edges being sampled, which are then used to compute normalization coefficients (α for aggregator normalization in feature propagation and λ for loss normalization). During training, an independent subgraph (Gs) is sampled in each iteration, a full GCN is built on Gs, and forward/backward propagation proceeds with the derived normalizations. To reduce variance, the paper analyzes independent edge sampling and derives an optimal edge probability proportional to the sum of aggregated features and their reciprocals, simplifying it to a topology-dependent form (pe ∝ 1/deg(u) + 1/deg(v)). Four specific samplers are proposed and integrated: Random Node Sampler, Random Edge Sampler, Random Walk (RW) Sampler, and Multi-dimensional Random Walk (MRW) Sampler, all of which induce a subgraph from the sampled elements to ensure connectivity.",
    "Experimental Setup": "GraphSAINT was evaluated under an inductive, supervised learning setting on five large-scale graphs for node classification tasks: PPI (protein functions), Flickr (image categorization), Reddit (online post communities), Yelp (business categorization), and Amazon (product categories). Datasets included both small and large versions of PPI, with varying sizes up to 1.5 million nodes and 132 million edges. Performance was measured using the F1-micro score. The method was compared against six state-of-the-art baselines: vanilla GCN, GraphSAGE, FastGCN, S-GCN, AS-GCN, and ClusterGCN. Experiments were conducted on a single machine with Dual Intel Xeon CPUs and an NVIDIA Tesla P100 GPU. Hyperparameters (hidden dimension, dropout, learning rate) were tuned via grid search, and the Adam optimizer was used. The sampler leveraged parallel execution on 40 CPU cores. Models were trained for a fixed number of epochs, with the best performing model on the validation set being reloaded for test set evaluation. Scalability on deeper models (up to 5 layers) and integration with GAT and JK-net architectures were also tested.",
    "Limitations": "The paper does not explicitly list limitations of GraphSAINT in a dedicated section. However, some inherent constraints or challenges can be inferred. The derivation of the optimal edge sampler (Theorem 3.2) makes a simplification by ignoring feature activations (x^(l)v) to make edge probabilities dependent solely on graph topology, which might sacrifice some theoretical optimality. The Multi-dimensional Random Walk (MRW) sampler is noted as being 'more expensive to execute' compared to other GraphSAINT samplers, implying a trade-off between its sampling quality and computational cost. Furthermore, when integrating GraphSAINT with Graph Attention Networks (GAT), explicit variance reduction becomes difficult due to the dynamically updated attention values, suggesting that its full optimization for such dynamic architectures might be complex. The necessity to remove GAT's softmax step for minibatch training also points to adaptations required for the framework.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The paper suggests two main future research directions. First, developing distributed training algorithms for GraphSAINT, where the training graph is partitioned across distributed memory, and sampling can be performed independently on each processor. This approach aims to leverage self-supportive subgraphs to significantly reduce system-level communication costs, necessitating the development of data shuffling strategies alongside specific graph samplers. Second, focusing on algorithm-system co-optimization to accelerate GraphSAINT's training on heterogeneous computing platforms (e.g., CPU-FPGA). This direction seeks to capitalize on GraphSAINT's resolution of the 'neighbor explosion' problem, which reduces computation complexity and improves hardware utilization, and its decoupled sampling and propagation steps that facilitate task-level parallelization.",
    "Experiment Code": "import numpy as np\nimport scipy.sparse\nimport time\nimport math\nimport pdb\nfrom math import ceil\nimport graphsaint.cython_sampler as cy\n\nclass GraphSampler:\n    def __init__(self, adj_train, node_train, size_subgraph, args_preproc):\n        self.adj_train = adj_train\n        self.node_train = np.unique(node_train).astype(np.int32)\n        self.size_subgraph = size_subgraph\n        self.name_sampler = 'None'\n        self.node_subgraph = None\n        self.preproc(**args_preproc)\n\n    def preproc(self, **kwargs):\n        pass\n\n    def par_sample(self, stage, **kwargs):\n        return self.cy_sampler.par_sample()\n\n    def _helper_extract_subgraph(self, node_ids):\n        node_ids = np.unique(node_ids)\n        node_ids.sort()\n        orig2subg = {n: i for i, n in enumerate(node_ids)}\n        n = node_ids.size\n        indptr = np.zeros(node_ids.size + 1)\n        indices = []\n        subg_edge_index = []\n        subg_nodes = node_ids\n        for nid in node_ids:\n            idx_s, idx_e = self.adj_train.indptr[nid], self.adj_train.indptr[nid + 1]\n            neighs = self.adj_train.indices[idx_s : idx_e]\n            for i_n, n in enumerate(neighs):\n                if n in orig2subg:\n                    indices.append(orig2subg[n])\n                    indptr[orig2subg[nid] + 1] += 1\n                    subg_edge_index.append(idx_s + i_n)\n        indptr = indptr.cumsum().astype(np.int64)\n        indices = np.array(indices)\n        subg_edge_index = np.array(subg_edge_index)\n        data = np.ones(indices.size)\n        assert indptr[-1] == indices.size == subg_edge_index.size\n        return indptr, indices, data, subg_nodes, subg_edge_index\n\nclass rw_sampling(GraphSampler):\n    def __init__(self, adj_train, node_train, size_subgraph, size_root, size_depth):\n        self.size_root = size_root\n        self.size_depth = size_depth\n        size_subgraph = size_root * size_depth\n NUM_PAR_SAMPLER,\n            SAMPLES_PER_PROC,\n            self.edge_prob_tri.row,\n            self.edge_prob_tri.col,\n            self.edge_prob_tri.data.cumsum(),\n            self.num_edges_subgraph,\n        )\n\n    def preproc(self,**kwargs):\n        self.edge_prob = scipy.sparse.csr_matrix(\n            (\n                np.zeros(self.adj_train.size),\n                self.adj_train.indices,\n                self.adj_train.indptr\n            ),\n            shape=self.adj_train.shape,\n        )\n        self.edge_prob.data[:] = self.adj_train_norm.data[:]\n        _adj_trans = scipy.sparse.csr_matrix.tocsc(self.adj_train_norm)\n        self.edge_prob.data += _adj_trans.data\n        self.edge_prob.data *= 2 * self.num_edges_subgraph / self.sample_coverage * self.node_train.size:\n                break\n        print()\n        num_subg = len(self.subgraphs_remaining_nodes)\n        for i in range(num_subg):\n            self.norm_aggr_train[self.subgraphs_remaining_edge_index[i]] += 1\n            self.norm_loss_train[self.subgraphs_remaining_nodes[i]] += 1\n        assert self.norm_loss_train[self.node_val].sum() + cpu_eval=False):\n        super(GraphSAINT,self).__init__()\n        self.use_cuda = (args_global.gpu >= 0)\n        if cpu_eval:\n            self.use_cuda=False\n        if \"attention\" in       _dims, self.order_layer, self.act_layer, self.bias_layer, self.aggr_layer \\\n                        = parse_layer_yml(arch_gcn, self.feat_full.shape[1])\n        self.set_idx_conv()\n _loss(self, preds, labels, norm_loss):\n        if self.sigmoid_loss:\n            norm_loss = norm_loss.unsqueeze(1)\n            return torch.nn.BCEWithLogitsLoss(weight=norm_loss,reduction='sum')(preds, labels)\n        else:\n            _ls = torch.nn.CrossEntropyLoss(reduction='none')(preds, labels)\n            return (norm_loss*_ls).sum()\n\n\ndef adj_norm(adj, deg=None, sort_indices=True):\n    diag_shape = (adj.shape[0],adj.shape[1])\n    D = adj.sum(1).flatten() if deg is None else deg\n    norm_diag = sp.dia_matrix((1/D,0),shape=diag_shape)\n    adj_norm = norm_diag.dot(adj)\n    if sort_indices:\n        adj_norm.sort_indices()\n    return adj_norm",
    "Experiment Result": "The GraphSAINT method employs various graph sampling techniques and normalization procedures. The experimental setup is highly configurable via command-line arguments and a YAML training configuration file.\n\n### Global Arguments (from `graphsaint/globals.py`):\n- `num_cpu_core`: Number of CPU cores for parallel sampling (default: 20).\n- `log_device_placement`: Whether to log device placement (default: False).\n- `data_prefix`: Prefix identifying training data (required).\n- `dir_log`: Base directory for logging and saving embeddings (default: \".\").\n- `gpu`: Which GPU to use (default: auto-detected, or -1 for CPU-only).\n- `eval_train_every`: How often to evaluate training subgraph accuracy (default: 15 batches).\n- `train_config`: Path to the YAML configuration of training (required).\n- `dtype`: Data type for floating point (s for single, d for double, default: s, currently only float32 supported).\n- `timeline`: To save timeline.json for profiling (default: False).\n- `tensorboard`: To save data to tensorboard (default: False).\n- `dualGPU`: Whether to distribute the model to two GPUs (default: False).\n- `cpu_eval`: Whether to use CPU for evaluation (default: False).\n- `saved_model_path`: Path to a pretrained model file (default: \"\").\n\n### Training Configuration (parsed by `graphsaint/utils.py:parse_n_prepare`):\nThe `parse_n_prepare` function loads `train_config` (a YAML file) and sets up the following:\n- **`arch_gcn`**: Dictionary defining the GNN architecture parameters.\n    - `dim`: Hidden dimension of GCN layers.\n    - `aggr`: Aggregation type ('concat' or 'mean').\n    - `loss`: Loss function type ('softmax' or 'sigmoid').\n    - `arch`: String defining layer orders (e.g., '1-0-1').\n    - `act`: Activation function (e.g., 'I', 'relu', 'leaky_relu').\n    - `bias`: Bias type ('norm', 'norm-nn', or 'bias').\n    - `attention`: Integer indicating number of attention heads (if GAT/GaAN layers are used).\n    - `gated_attention`: Boolean, true if using Gated Attention.\n- **`train_params`**: Dictionary for training hyperparameters.\n    - `lr`: Learning rate.\n    - `weight_decay`: L2 regularization coefficient.\n    - `norm_loss`: Boolean, whether to use loss normalization.\n    - `norm_aggr`: Boolean, whether to use aggregator normalization.\n    - `q_threshold`, `q_offset`: Parameters for adaptive quantization (default: 50, 0).\n    - `sample_coverage`: A ratio (e.g., 50) indicating how many times the total number of training nodes should be sampled during the warm-up phase to estimate normalization coefficients.\n    - `eval_val_every`: How often to evaluate on the validation set (in epochs).\n- **`train_phases`**: A list of dictionaries, each defining a training phase with its sampling strategy and duration. Each phase dict typically includes:\n    - `end`: The epoch at which this phase ends.\n    - `sampler`: The type of graph sampler to use ('mrw', 'rw', 'edge', 'node', 'full_batch', 'vanilla_node_python').\n    - Specific parameters for the chosen sampler, such as `size_subgraph`, `num_root`, `depth`, `size_frontier`, `deg_clip`, `size_subg_edge`.\n\n### Training Procedure (from `graphsaint/pytorch_version/train.py:train`):\n1.  **Phase Iteration**: The training iterates through each defined `train_phase`.\n2.  **Sampler and Normalization Setup**: For each phase, `minibatch.set_sampler(phase)` is called. This initializes the `GraphSampler` (e.g., `mrw_sampling`, `edge_sampling`) specified for the phase and performs a 'warm-up' sampling. During this warm-up, a sufficient number of subgraphs are sampled (controlled by `sample_coverage`) to estimate the `norm_loss_train` (λ, loss normalization) for each training node and `norm_aggr_train` (α, aggregator normalization) for each edge in the training graph.\n3.  **Epoch Iteration**: Training proceeds for a specified number of epochs within each phase.\n4.  **Minibatch Generation**: In each epoch, `minibatch.shuffle()` randomizes the order of training nodes. Then, `minibatch.one_batch(mode='train')` is called to retrieve a subgraph. This method either pops a pre-sampled subgraph from a buffer or initiates a new batch of parallel subgraph sampling if the buffer is empty. The retrieved subgraph's adjacency matrix has its edge data modified by `norm_aggr_train` (via `norm_aggr` utility), and the corresponding `norm_loss_train` values for the subgraph nodes are also retrieved.\n5.  **Model Training**: `model.train_step()` executes forward and backward propagation. Inside, `GraphSAINT._loss` uses the `norm_loss_subgraph` (λ values) to weight the contribution of each node's loss to the total batch loss. The normalized adjacency matrix (which includes α via `adj_norm` and `norm_aggr` utilities) is used in the GCN layers for feature propagation.\n6.  **Evaluation**: Training accuracy is evaluated after `args_global.eval_train_every` batches. Full-batch validation is performed every `eval_val_every` epochs, and the model with the best validation F1-micro score is saved. A final test set evaluation is conducted with this best model after all training phases conclude.\n\n### Adjacency Normalization (`graphsaint/utils.py:adj_norm`):\nThis utility function normalizes an adjacency matrix using random walk (row) normalization. It adds self-connections implicitly (by `adj.sum(1)`) to compute degrees and then multiplies by the inverse degree matrix (D^-1 * adj)."
}{
    "Title": "SAC: Accelerating and Structuring Self-Attention via Sparse Adaptive Connection",
    "Main Contributions": "The main research problem addressed is the quadratic memory and computational complexity of the self-attention mechanism, which limits its applicability to long input sequences, and the sub-optimal performance stemming from pre-defined, fully-connected attention structures. This paper introduces Sparse Adaptive Connection (SAC), a novel method that accelerates and structures self-attention by dynamically learning sparse attention edges. Key contributions include proposing an LSTM-based Edge Predictor that constructs attention links optimized for task-specific performance, thereby reducing complexity from quadratic to linear (O(αN) where α is the average number of edges per node), effectively selecting salient nodes, and significantly reducing memory cost. SAC is demonstrated to be competitive with state-of-the-art models across diverse tasks such as neural machine translation, language modeling, graph representation learning, and image classification, while also showing that many previous self-attention variants can be seen as special cases of SAC.",
    "Methodology": "SAC operates by replacing the conventional fully-connected self-attention structure with a sparse, adaptively learned graph-like structure. The core of SAC is an LSTM-based Edge Predictor that sequentially constructs edges between nodes. Edge prediction is decoupled into identifying an original node and a destination node, with the LSTM taking the representation of the previously predicted node as input and using a softmax function for subsequent node prediction. The entire edge construction process is optimized using reinforcement learning (REINFORCE), where the reward is the log probability of predicting the correct label for the downstream task. To incorporate inherent structural information (e.g., relative distances in text, existing graph edges), distance encodings are integrated into the edge predictor by adding a learnable distance matrix to the node projection matrix. SAC allows for variations such as sharing the same edge structure across all layers (adopted in the experiments for search space reduction), enforcing all nodes to be connected by only predicting destination nodes, or utilizing head-adaptive edge predictors for distinct attention heads. At test time, edges are decoded using beam search.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The SAC model was extensively evaluated across four distinct tasks: 1. Neural Machine Translation (NMT): Used the WMT 2014 English-German dataset with a Transformer encoder-decoder backbone, BPE encoding, Adam optimizer, and label smoothing. Performance was measured by BLEU scores on newstest2013 (dev) and newstest2014 (test), with varying α (average edges per node) from 2 to 15. 2. Language Modeling: Tested on character-level Enwiki8 and Text8 datasets using a Transformer decoder, allowing for a maximum context of 50k characters with α=256. Optimization was performed using Adagrad. Performance was reported in Bits Per Character (BPC). 3. Graph Representation Learning: Evaluated on transductive (Cora, Citeseer, Pubmed) and inductive (Protein-Protein Interaction, PPI) datasets. SAC was initialized using a pretrained Graph Attention Network (GAT) model, with 8 attention heads and 12 blocks. Different α values ([5, 10, 50, 100]) were explored. Adam optimizer with early stopping based on loss/accuracy (transductive) or micro-F1 (inductive) was used. 4. Image Classification: Conducted on CIFAR-100 (using Wide-ResNet-28-10) and ImageNet (using ResNet-50) backbones, augmenting residual blocks with self-attention (16 heads) and incorporating relative position embeddings. α was tuned in {5, 10, 20}. Performance was measured by Top-1 and Top-5 accuracy.",
    "Limitations": "The paper implicitly indicates a limitation in that directly training the edge predictor is impractical without ground-truth edges, necessitating the use of reinforcement learning. While reinforcement learning enables the model to learn optimal sparse connections, it introduces complexity, particularly in the search space. Specifically, the head-adaptive strategy, which allows different attention heads to learn distinct attention patterns, significantly increases this search space and did not consistently provide significant performance boosts across all evaluated tasks (e.g., NMT). This suggests that while promising, the head-adaptive variant may require further refinements or more targeted application scenarios to yield consistent benefits.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The paper suggests that SAC holds significant potential for future research in generating high-quality long text. This implies that subsequent work could explore applying SAC to advanced generative tasks that require processing and producing extended sequences, leveraging its ability to handle long-range dependencies efficiently and automatically structure attention patterns, which could lead to more coherent and fluent long-form content generation.",
    "Experiment Code": null,

    "Title": "O(n) Connections are Expressive Enough: Universal Approximability of Sparse Transformers",
    "Main Contributions": "This paper addresses the quadratic computational cost of dense Transformer networks for long input sequences and investigates the expressive power of sparse Transformers. The main contributions include proposing a unified framework that generalizes existing sparse attention models, establishing sufficient conditions (Assumptions 1 and 2) under which sparse Transformers with O(n) connections per attention layer are universal approximators of any continuous sequence-to-sequence function, and providing theoretical evidence that O(n) connections are expressive enough. The work also presents experimental results comparing different sparsity patterns and levels on standard NLP tasks.",
    "Methodology": "The authors propose a unifying framework for sparse Transformers by modifying the standard Transformer block to a Sparse Transformer Block (STB). This STB incorporates cycling sparsity patterns, denoted by {Al k}, and generalizes the probability map ρ (e.g., softmax) applied after attention score computation. The core theoretical methodology involves proving a universal approximation theorem based on two key assumptions: 1) **Assumption 1 (Sparsity Patterns)** ensures that tokens attend to themselves, there's a Hamiltonian path for information flow, and all tokens can indirectly attend to each other within a finite 's' steps. 2) **Assumption 2 (Probability Map)** requires ρ to approximate the hardmax operator, which is shown to hold for softmax, sparselin-gen, and α-entmax. The proof strategy involves approximating the target function with a piecewise constant function, then constructing a modified sparse Transformer (using hardmax and piecewise linear activations) to exactly represent it, and finally approximating this modified model with a standard sparse Transformer while carefully bounding the accumulation of approximation errors across layers. This includes developing 'sparse selective shift' and 'all-max-shift' operations.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experimental study compared different sparsity patterns and levels across four tasks: 1) **Synthetic Copying Task**: Input '0s0s' with 127-length 's' sequences, max sequence length 256. Models had 1-4 attention layers, 4 heads (size 64), embedding dim 256. 2) **Language Modeling**: One Billion Word Benchmark, max sequence length 256. Used a 12-block Transformer, 8 heads (size 64), embedding dim 256. 3) **Translation**: WMT18 English-Czech and English-German/German-English datasets, max sequence length 256. Used a 6-block Transformer, 8 heads (size 32). 4) **GLUE Tasks**: MNLI, XNLI, CoLA, and MRPC using a BERTBASE model, max sequence length 128. \nSparsity patterns included STRIDED, FIXED, STAR, and RANDOM, with head configurations SEQUENTIAL, UNION, and MULTIHEAD. Training involved AdamW (copying) or Adafactor (LM/Translation) optimizers, with specific warmup and total training steps. Metrics were prediction accuracy (copying, GLUE), perplexity (LM), and BLEU score (translation). Experiments utilized 8 TPUv3 or TPUv2 chips.",
    "Limitations": "The universal approximation theorem holds for a fixed sequence length 'n' and applies to the encoder part of the Transformer network. The experimental results indicate that the optimal design of sparsity patterns is highly task-dependent; for instance, the STAR pattern performed well in language modeling but struggled in copying, translation, and BERT experiments. Similarly, the UNION head configuration showed strong performance in language modeling and translation but suffered in BERT tasks. The theoretical guarantees rely on specific width constraints (h=2, m=1, r=4) for the sparse Transformer.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The work opens avenues for further theoretical understanding of sparse Transformers, particularly for scenarios with varying sequence lengths, complementing existing research on the limitations of Transformers in such dynamic settings. Given the task-dependent nature of optimal sparsity patterns observed in experiments, future research could focus on developing more systematic guidance or adaptive methods for designing or learning effective sparsity patterns that can generalize across diverse NLP tasks. The paper also encourages further investigation into the implications of the derived universal approximation properties for practical sparse attention model design.",
    "Experiment Code": null,

    "Title": "Even Sparser Graph Transformers",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the scalability issues of Graph Transformers, which typically suffer from quadratic memory complexity. It proposes Spexphormer, a two-phase training procedure that significantly reduces memory requirements while maintaining competitive accuracy. The key idea is to first train a narrow network to estimate attention scores, then use these scores to sparsify the graph for training a wider, more powerful network. Theoretical conditions are established for when a narrow network's attention scores can approximate those of a wide network, and the method's effectiveness is demonstrated across various graph datasets with drastically reduced memory consumption.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology, Spexphormer, involves a two-phase training process. The first phase trains an 'Attention Score Estimator Network,' a narrow-width (e.g., 4-8) network with one attention head, designed to learn important neighbor connections. This network uses a higher-degree expander graph (30-200) and learnable embeddings for different edge types. Two architectural changes enhance consistency: V-normalization with a learnable global scale and a variable temperature annealing schedule (starting at 1.0, gradually reducing to 0.05) to sharpen attention scores. The second phase uses the learned attention scores from the first network to create a layer-wise sparsified interaction graph by sampling a fixed number of edges (degree 'degℓ') per node per layer, without replacement. This sampling is task-dependent and utilizes reservoir sampling for GPU-friendly, parallel execution. The final, wider network is then trained on this much sparser graph, benefiting from regular matrix calculations due to fixed node degrees. The model also incorporates a novel batching technique based on neighborhood expansion to efficiently process large graphs.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on twelve medium-sized graphs (six homophilic: CS, Physics, Photo, Computer, WikiCS, ogbn-arxiv; and six heterophilic: Minesweeper, Tolokers, Roman-Empire, Amazon-Ratings, Questions, Actor) and three large graphs (ogbn-proteins, Amazon2M, Pokec). Standard train/validation/test splits were used for most datasets, with some custom splits for Actor (50%/25%/25%) and specific large datasets (Amazon2M, Pokec). Performance metrics included accuracy for most datasets and ROC-AUC for Minesweeper, Tolokers, Questions, and ogbn-proteins. Baselines included GCN, GraphSAGE, GAT, GraphSAINT, Nodeformer, Difformer, SGFormer, GraphGPS, GOAT, GloGNN, SGC, NAGphormer, Exphormer, SIGN, and MLP. Ablation studies assessed the impact of uniform/max attention sampling, constant temperature, and absence of layer normalization. Hardware for training consisted of A100 (40GB) and V100 (32GB/16GB) GPUs, with CPU devices (Intel Xeon E5-2680 v4, 500GB RAM) for large graph attention score estimation.",
    "Limitations": "The primary limitation is the reliance on large CPU memory for the attention score estimation phase, especially for extremely large datasets. While this step is performed only once per dataset and network depth, it still requires substantial computational resources (e.g., 200GB to 500GB RAM for current large datasets). For datasets beyond this scale, highly distributed computation would be necessary. Additionally, the experimental analysis showed that selecting only the top-k attention scores may not be fully representative in transductive learning on graphs, possibly because attention can be distributed across many similar nodes rather than concentrated on a few, which could affect the approximation power of the sparse graph.",
    "Future Research Directions": "A promising future research direction is to combine the sampling process with simultaneous, dynamic attention score estimation, which could help scale the initial estimation phase to even larger graphs without requiring vast CPU memory. Another potential area stems from the insight that attention scores in layers after the first are very similar; this could allow for training initial networks with fewer layers and repeating attention scores from the final layer to construct deeper Spexphormer models.",
    "Experiment Code": "import torch\nimport torch.nn as nn\nimport torch.nn.functional as F\nfrom torch_geometric.graphgym.config import cfg\nfrom torch_geometric.graphgym.register import register_layer\nfrom pathlib import Path\nimport numpy as np\n\n\nclass ASE_Attention_Layer(nn.Module):\n\n    def __init__(self, in_dim, out_dim, num_heads, layer_idx, use_bias=False, dim_edge=None, use_virt_nodes=False):\n        super().__init__()\n\n        self.out_dim = out_dim // num_heads\n        self.num_heads = num_heads\n        self.use_virt_nodes = use_virt_nodes\n        self.use_bias = use_bias\n        self.layer_idx = layer_idx\n\n        if dim_edge is None:\n            dim_edge = in_dim\n\n        self.QKV = nn.Linear(in_dim, self.out_dim * num_heads * 3, bias=use_bias)\n        self.V_scale = nn.Parameter(data=torch.Tensor([0.25]), requires_grad=True)\n\n        self.use_edge_attr = cfg.gt.use_edge_feats\n        if self.use_edge_attr:\n            self.E1 = nn.Linear(dim_edge, self.out_dim * num_heads, bias=use_bias)\n            self.E2 = nn.Linear(dim_edge, num_heads, bias=True)\n\n        self.T = 1.0\n\n    def propagate_attention(self, batch, edge_index):\n        src = batch.K_h[edge_index[0].to(torch.long)]\n        dest = batch.Q_h[edge_index[1].to(torch.long)]\n        score = torch.einsum('ehd,ehd->eh', src, dest)\n\n        score = score / np.sqrt(self.out_dim)\n\n        if self.use_edge_attr:\n            score = score * batch.E.sum(-1)\n            score = score.unsqueeze(-1) + batch.E2\n        else:\n            score = score.unsqueeze(-1)\n\n        score = score / self.T\n        score = torch.exp(score.clamp(-8, 8))\n\n\n        msg = batch.V_h[edge_index[0].to(torch.long)] * score\n        \n        batch.wV = torch.zeros_like(batch.V_h)\n        batch.wV.index_add_(0, edge_index[1].to(torch.long), msg)\n\n        batch.Z = score.new_zeros(batch.V_h.size(0), self.num_heads, 1)\n        batch.Z.index_add_(0, edge_index[1].to(torch.long), score)\n\n        if cfg.train.saving_epoch:\n            new_score = score/(batch.Z[edge_index[1]])\n            score_np = new_score.cpu().detach().numpy()\n            Path(f'Attention_scores/{cfg.dataset.name}').mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\n            with open(f'Attention_scores/{cfg.dataset.name}/seed{cfg.seed}_h{self.out_dim}_layer_{self.layer_idx}.npy', 'wb') as f:\n                np.save(f, score_np)\n\n\n    def forward(self, batch):\n        if cfg.train.cur_epoch >= cfg.train.temp_wait:\n            self.T = max(cfg.train.temp_min, cfg.train.temp_rdc_ratio ** (cfg.train.cur_epoch - cfg.train.temp_wait))\n        edge_attr = batch.edge_attr\n        edge_index = batch.edge_index\n        h = batch.x\n        num_node = batch.batch.shape[0]\n        \n        QKV_h = self.QKV(h).view(-1, self.num_heads, 3 * self.out_dim)\n        batch.Q_h, batch.K_h, batch.V_h = torch.split(QKV_h, self.out_dim, dim=-1)\n        batch.V_h = F.normalize(batch.V_h, p=2.0, dim=-1) * self.V_scale\n\n        if self.use_edge_attr:\n            if cfg.dataset.edge_encoder_name == 'TypeDictEdge2':\n                E = self.E1(batch.edge_embeddings)[batch.edge_attr]\n                E2 = self.E2(batch.edge_embeddings)[batch.edge_attr]\n            else:\n                E = self.E1(edge_attr)\n                E2 = self.E2(edge_attr)\n            \n            batch.E = E.view(-1, self.num_heads, self.out_dim)\n            batch.E2 = E2.view(-1, self.num_heads, 1)\n\n        self.propagate_attention(batch, edge_index)\n\n        h_out = batch.wV / (batch.Z + 1e-6)\n\n        h_out = h_out.view(-1, self.out_dim * self.num_heads)\n\n        if self.use_virt_nodes:\n            batch.virt_h = h_out[num_node:]\n            h_out = h_out[:num_node]\n\n        return h_out\n\n\nregister_layer('ASE_Attention_Layer',      batch = module(batch)\n        return batch\n\n\nimport torch\nfrom torch_geometric.graphgym.config import cfg\nfrom torch_geometric.graphgym.register import (register_node_encoder,\n                                               register_edge_encoder)\n\n\n@register_edge_encoder('TypeDictEdge')\nclass TypeDictEdgeEncoder(torch.nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, emb_dim):\n degree, rng=None):\n  if rng is None:\n    rng = np.random.default_rng()\n\n  senders = [*range(0, num_nodes)] * degree\n  receivers = rng.permutation(senders).tolist()\n\n  senders, receivers = [*senders, *receivers], [*receivers, *senders]\n\n  return senders, receivers\n\n\ndef generate_random_regular_graph1(num_nodes, degree, rng=None):\n  if rng is None:\n    rng = np.random.default_rng()\n\n  senders = [*range(0, num_nodes)] * degree\n  receivers = []\n  for _ in range(degree):\n    receivers.extend(rng.permutation(list(range(num_nodes))).tolist())\n\n  senders, receivers = [*senders, *receivers], [*receivers, *senders]\n\n  senders = np.array(senders)\n  receivers = np.array(receivers)\n\n  return senders, receivers\n\n\ndef generate_random_graph_with_hamiltonian_cycles(num_nodes, degree, rng=None):\n  if rng is None:\n    rng = np.random.default_rng()\n\n  senders = []\n  receivers = []\n  for _ in range(degree):\n    permutation = rng.permutation(list(range(num_nodes))).tolist()\n    for idx, v in enumerate(permutation):\n      u = permutation[idx - 1]\n      senders.extend([v, u])\n      receivers.extend([u, v])\n\n  senders = np.array(senders)\n  receivers = np.array(receivers)\n\n  return senders, receivers\n\n\ndef augment_with_expander(data, degree, algorithm, rng=None, max_num_iters=100, exp_index=0):\n  num_nodes = data.num_nodes\n\n  if rng is None:\n    rng = np.random.default_rng()\n  \n  eig_val = -1\n  eig_val_lower_bound = max(0, 2 * degree - 2 * math.sqrt(2 * degree - 1) - 0.1)\n\n  max_eig_val_so_far = -1\n  max_senders = []\n  max_receivers = []\n  cur_iter = 1\n\n  if num_nodes <= degree:\n    degree = num_nodes - 1    \n    \n  if num_nodes <= 10:\n    for i in range(num_nodes):\n      for j in range(num_nodes):      \n        if i != j:\n          max_senders.append(i)\n          max_receivers.append(j)\n  else:\n    while eig_val < eig_val_lower_bound and cur_iter <= max_num_iters:\n      if algorithm == 'Random-d':\n        senders, receivers = generate_random_regular_graph1(num_nodes, degree, rng)\n      elif algorithm == 'Random-d-2':\n        senders, receivers = generate_random_regular_graph2(num_nodes, degree, rng)\n      elif algorithm == 'Hamiltonian':\n        senders, receivers = generate_random_graph_with_hamiltonian_cycles(num_nodes, degree, rng)\n      else:\n        raise ValueError('prep.exp_algorithm should be one of the Random-d or Hamiltonian')\n      \n      if num_nodes > 1e5:\n        max_senders = senders\n        max_receivers = receivers\n        break\n\n      [eig_val, _] = laplacian_eigenv(senders, receivers, k=1, n=num_nodes)\n      if len(eig_val) == 0:\n        print(\"num_nodes = %d, degree = %d, cur_iter = %d, mmax_iters = %d, senders = %d, receivers = %d\" %(num_nodes, degree, cur_iter, max_num_iters, len(senders), len(receivers)))\n        eig_val = 0\n      else:\n        eig_val = eig_val[0]\n\n      if eig_val > max_eig_val_so_far:\n        max_eig_val_so_far = eig_val\n        max_senders = senders\n        max_receivers = receivers\n\n      cur_iter += 1\n\n  non_loops = [\n      *filter(lambda i: max_senders[i] != max_receivers[i], range(0, len(max_senders)))\n  ]\n\n  senders = np.array(max_senders)[non_loops]\n  receivers = np.array(max_receivers)[non_loops]\n\n  max_senders = torch.tensor(max_senders, dtype=torch.long).view(-1, 1)\n  max_receivers = torch.tensor(max_receivers, dtype=torch.long).view(-1, 1)\n  expander_edges = torch.cat([max_senders, max_receivers], dim=1)\n\n\n  data.edge_index = torch.cat([data.edge_index, expander_edges.t()], dim=1)\n  num_exp_edges = expander_edges.shape[0]\n\n  edge_type = torch.zeros(data.edge_index.shape[1], dtype=torch.long)\n  edge_type[-num_exp_edges:] = 1\n\n  num_nodes = data.num_nodes\n  self_loops = torch.arange(num_nodes, dtype=torch.long).unsqueeze(0).repeat(2, 1)\n  data.edge_index = torch.cat((data.edge_index, self_loops), dim=1)\n  self_loop_feats = torch.full((num_nodes,), 2, dtype=torch.long)\n  edge_type = torch.cat((edge_type, self_loop_feats))\n\n  edge_type = edge_type.contiguous()\n\n  if hasattr(data, 'edge_attr') and data.edge_attr is not None:\n    num_new_edges = edge_type.shape[0] - data.edge_attr.shape[0]\n    new_edge_attr = torch.zeros((num_new_edges, data.edge_attr.shape[1]), dtype=data.edge_attr.dtype)\n    data.edge_attr = torch.cat((data.edge_attr, new_edge_attr), dim=0)\n    edge_type_attr = edge_type\n    data.edge_attr = torch.cat((edge_type_attr, data.edge_attr), dim=1)\n  else:\n    data.edge_attr = edge_type\n\n  if cfg.prep.save_edges:\n    edge_set_path = Path(f'EdgeSets/{cfg.dataset.name}')\n    edge_set_path.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\n    np.save(edge_set_path / 'edges_exp.npy', data.edge_index.cpu().detach().numpy())\n    np.save(edge_set_path / 'edge_attr_exp.npy', data.edge_attr.cpu().detach().numpy())\n  else:\n    if exp_index == 0:\n      data.expander_edges = expander_edges\n    else:\n      attrname = f\"expander_edges{exp_index}\"\n      setattr(data, attrname, expander_edges)\n    \n      \n  return data\n\n\nimport logging\nimport time\nimport os\n\nimport numpy as np\nimport torch\n\nfrom (other code for training loop) ...\n    # Log current best stats on eval epoch.\n    if is_eval_epoch(cur_epoch):\n        # ... (other code) ...\n\n    if cfg.train.save_attention_scores:\n        cfg.train.saving_epoch=True\n        load_ckpt(model)\n        model.eval()\n        cfg.train.cur_epoch = int(best_epoch)\n        os.makedirs(f'Attention_scores/{cfg.dataset.name}', exist_ok=True)\n        with open(f'Attention_scores/{cfg.dataset.name}/edges.npy', 'wb') as f:\n            np.save(f, loaders[0].dataset.data.edge_index.detach().numpy()) \n        with open(f'Attention_scores/{cfg.dataset.name}/edge_attr.npy', 'wb') as f:\n            np.save(f, loaders[0].dataset.data.edge_attr.detach().numpy()) \n        with torch.no_grad():\n            for batch in loaders[1]:\n                batch.split = 'val'\n                batch.to(torch.device(cfg.accelerator))\n                _ = model(batch)\n        cfg.train.saving_epoch=False\n\n    # ... (rest of the function)\n\n\n\nimport torch\nimport torch.nn as nn\nimport torch.nn.functional as F\nfrom torch_geometric.graphgym.config import cfg\nfrom torch_geometric.graphgym.register import register_layer\n\n\nclass SpexphormerAttention(nn.Module):\n\n    def __init__(self, in_dim, out_dim, num_heads, layer_idx, use_bias=False):\n        super().__init__()\n\n        if out_dim % num_heads != 0:\n            raise ValueError('hidden dimension is not dividable by the number of heads')\n        self.out_dim = out_dim // num_heads\n        self.num_heads = num_heads\n        self.layer_idx = layer_idx\n\n        self.edge_index_name = f'edge_index_layer_{layer_idx}'\n        self.edge_attr_name = f'edge_type_layer_{layer_idx}'\n\n        self.Q = nn.Linear(in_dim, self.out_dim * num_heads, bias=use_bias)\n        self.K = nn.Linear(in_dim, self.out_dim * num_heads, bias=use_bias)\n        self.E1 = nn.Linear(in_dim, self.out_dim * num_heads, bias=use_bias)\n        self.E2 = nn.Linear(in_dim, num_heads, bias=True)\n        self.V = nn.Linear(in_dim, self.out_dim * num_heads, bias=use_bias)\n\n    def forward(self, batch):\n        edge_index = getattr(batch, self.edge_index_name)\n        edge_attr = getattr(batch, self.edge_attr_name)\n        \n        n1 = batch.num_layer_nodes[self.layer_idx].item()\n        n2 = batch.num_layer_nodes[self.layer_idx + 1].item()\n        assert batch.x.shape[0] == n1\n\n        Q_h = self.Q(batch.x[:n2]).view(-1, self.num_heads, self.out_dim)\n        K_h = self.K(batch.x).view(-1, self.num_heads, self.out_dim)\n        V_h = self.V(batch.x).view(-1, self.num_heads, self.out_dim)\n\n        if cfg.dataset.edge_encoder_name == 'TypeDictEdge2':\n            E1 = self.E1(batch.edge_embeddings)[edge_attr].view(n2, -1, self.num_heads, self.out_dim)\n            E2 = self.E2(batch.edge_embeddings)[edge_attr].view(n2, -1, self.num_heads, 1)\n        else:\n            E1 = self.E1(edge_attr).view(n2, -1, self.num_heads, self.out_dim)\n            E2 = self.E2(edge_attr).view(n2, -1, self.num_heads, 1)\n        \n        neighbors = edge_index[0, :]\n        deg = neighbors.shape[0]//n2\n        neighbors = neighbors.reshape(n2, deg)\n        \n        K_h = K_h[neighbors]\n        V_h = V_h[neighbors]\n        \n        score = torch.mul(E1, K_h)\n        \n        score = torch.bmm(score.view(-1, deg, self.out_dim), Q_h.view(-1, self.out_dim, 1))\n        score = score.view(-1, self.num_heads, deg)\n\n        score = score + E2.squeeze(-1).permute([0, 2, 1])\n        score = score.clamp(-8, 8)\n        score = F.softmax(score, dim=-1)\n        \n        V_h = V_h.permute(0, 2, 1, 3)\n        score = score.unsqueeze(-1)\n        h_out = torch.mul(score, V_h)\n        h_out = h_out.sum(dim=2)\n        h_out = h_out.reshape(n2, -1)\n\n        return h_out\n\n\nregister_layer('ExphormerFinal', SpexphormerAttention)\nregister_layer('ExphormerSecond', SpexphormerAttention)\nregister_layer('ExphormerRegularGraph', SpexphormerAttention)\n\n\nimport numpy as np\nimport pandas as pd\nimport torch\nfrom torch_geometric.data import Data\nfrom bottleneck import argpartition\n\ndef sampler_uniform(adj_eidx, P, P_inv, k):\n    deg = adj_eidx.shape[0]\n    if k > deg:\n        extra = np.random.choice(adj_eidx, size=k - deg, replace=True)\n        return np.concatenate([adj_eidx, extra])\n    idx = np.random.choice(adj_eidx, size=k, replace=False)\n    return idx\n\ndef sampler_reservoir(adj_eidx, P, P_inv, k):\n    deg = adj_eidx.shape[0]\n    if k > deg:\n        extra = np.random.choice(adj_eidx, size=k - deg, replace=True, p=P)\n        return np.concatenate([adj_eidx, extra])\n    rsv = -np.log(np.random.rand(deg)) * P_inv\n    idx = argpartition(rsv, k-1)[:k]\n    return adj_eidx[idx]\n\ndef sampler_get_max(adj_eidx, P, P_inv, k):\n    deg = adj_eidx.shape[0]\n    if k > deg:\n        extra = np.random.choice(adj_eidx, size=k - deg, replace=True, p=P)\n        return np.concatenate([adj_eidx, extra])\n    idx = argpartition(P, P.shape[0]-k)[P.shape[0]-k:]\n    return adj_eidx[idx]\n\n\nclass NeighborSampler():\n    def __init__(self, original_graph, edge_index, edge_attr, P, adj_eidx, deg, num_layers, sampler='graph_reservoir') -> None:\n        self.data = original_graph\n        self.edge_index, self.edge_attr, self.P, self.adj_eidx, self.deg, self.num_layers = \\\n            edge_index, edge_attr, P, adj_eidx, deg, num_layers\n        \n        self.sampler = sampler\n        self.P = tuple(self.P)\n        self.P_inv = tuple([1.0/(p+1e-8) for p in self.P])\n\n        if self.sampler in ['graph_reservoir', 'graph_uniform']:\n            self.sample_on_batching = False\n            self.sampled_edge_ids = []\n            self.all_nodes_neighbor_sampler()\n        else:\n            self.sample_on_batching = True\n\n    \n    def expand_neighborhood_layer(self, layer_nodes, layer_idx):\n        k = self.deg[layer_idx]\n        \n        if self.sample_on_batching:\n            if self.sampler == 'batch_reservoir':\n                layer_edge_ids = [sampler_reservoir(self.adj_eidx[v], self.P[v][layer_idx], self.P_inv[v][layer_idx], k) for v in layer_nodes]\n            elif self.sampler == 'batch_uniform':\n                layer_edge_ids = [sampler_uniform(self.adj_eidx[v], self.P[v][layer_idx], self.P_inv[v][layer_idx], k) for v in layer_nodes]\n            else:\n                raise ValueError(f'Unkown sampler {self.sampler}')\n            layer_edge_ids = np.concatenate(layer_edge_ids)\n        else:\n            layer_edge_ids = self.sampled_edge_ids[layer_idx][layer_nodes].flatten()\n\n        layer_edge_index = self.edge_index[:, layer_edge_ids]\n\n        new_nodes = layer_edge_index[0, :].squeeze()\n        new_layer_nodes = np.concatenate((layer_nodes, new_nodes))\n        new_layer_nodes = pd.unique(new_layer_nodes)\n        layer_edge_index = torch.from_numpy(layer_edge_index)\n\n        return new_layer_nodes, layer_edge_index, layer_edge_ids\n    \n\n    def make_batch(self, core_nodes):\n        edges = []\n        nodes = []\n        edge_ids = []\n        layer_nodes = np.array(core_nodes)\n        for layer_idx in reversed(range(self.num_layers)):\n            layer_nodes, layer_edges, layer_edge_ids = self.expand_neighborhood_layer(layer_nodes, layer_idx)\n            edges.append(layer_edges)\n            nodes.append(layer_nodes)\n            edge_ids.append(layer_edge_ids)\n\n        edges = edges[::-1]\n        nodes = nodes[::-1]\n        edge_ids = edge_ids[::-1]\n        data = Data(x=self.data.x[nodes[0]])\n\n        max_idx = np.max(nodes[0])\n        index_mapping = torch.zeros(max_idx+1, dtype=torch.int)\n        index_mapping[nodes[0]] = torch.arange(nodes[0].shape[0], dtype=torch.int)\n        for l in range(self.num_layers):\n            mapped_edge_indexes = index_mapping[edges[l]].long()\n            setattr(data, f'edge_index_layer_{l}', mapped_edge_indexes)\n            setattr(data, f'edge_type_layer_{l}', self.edge_attr[edge_ids[l]])\n\n        num_layer_nodes = [len(layer_nodes) for layer_nodes in nodes]\n        num_layer_nodes.append(len(core_nodes))\n        data.num_layer_nodes = torch.Tensor(num_layer_nodes).int()\n        data.y = self.data.y[core_nodes]\n\n        return data\n\n\n    def all_nodes_neighbor_sampler(self, layer_node_deg=None):\n        if layer_node_deg is None:\n            layer_node_deg = self.deg\n        self.sampled_edge_ids = []\n        num_nodes = len(self.P)\n        for layer_idx in range(self.num_layers):\n            if self.sampler == 'graph_reservoir':\n                layer_edge_ids = [sampler_reservoir(self.adj_eidx[v], self.P[v][layer_idx], self.P_inv[v][layer_idx], layer_node_deg[layer_idx]) for v in range(num_nodes)]\n            elif self.sampler == 'graph_max':\n                layer_edge_ids = [sampler_get_max(self.adj_eidx[v], self.P[v][layer_idx], self.P_inv[v][layer_idx], layer_node_deg[layer_idx]) for v in range(num_nodes)]\n            elif self.sampler == 'graph_uniform':\n                layer_edge_ids = [sampler_uniform(self.adj_eidx[v], self.P[v][layer_idx], self.P_inv[v][layer_idx], layer_node_deg[layer_idx]) for v in range(num_nodes)]\n            else:\n                raise ValueError(f'unknown neighbor sampler {self.sampler}')\n            layer_edge_ids = np.stack(layer_edge_ids)\n            self.sampled_edge_ids.append(layer_edge_ids)\n\n\nimport logging\nimport time\n\nimport numpy as np\nimport torch\nimport random\n\nfrom torch_geometric.graphgym.checkpoint import load_ckpt\nfrom torch_geometric.graphgym.config import cfg\nfrom torch_geometric.graphgym.loss import compute_loss\nfrom torch_geometric.graphgym.register import register_train\nfrom torch_geometric.graphgym.utils.epoch import is_eval_epoch\n\nfrom spexphormer.train.neighbor_sampler import NeighborSampler\n\n\ndef train_epoch(logger, loader, model, optimizer, scheduler, batch_accumulation, neighbor_sampler):\n    model.train()\n    optimizer.zero_grad()\n    time_start = time.time()\n\n    train_nodes = torch.where(loader.dataset.train_mask)[0].tolist()\n    random.shuffle(train_nodes)\n    batch_size = cfg.train.batch_size\n\n    n = len(train_nodes)\n    num_iter = n//batch_size\n    if n % batch_size:\n        num_iter += 1\n    for iter in 1) % batch_accumulation == 0) or (iter + 1 == len(loader)):\n            if cfg.optim.clip_grad_norm:\n                torch.nn.utils.clip_grad_norm_(model.parameters(), 1.0)\n            optimizer.step()\n            optimizer.zero_grad()\n        logger.update_stats(true=_true,\n                            pred=_pred,\n                            loss=loss.detach().cpu().item(),\n                            lr=scheduler.get_last_lr()[0],\n                            time_used=time.time() - time_start,\n                            params=cfg.params,\n                            dataset_name=cfg.dataset.name)\n        time_start = time.time()\n\n\n@torch.no_grad()\ndef eval_epoch(logger, loader, model, neighbor_sampler, split='val'):\n    model.eval()\n    time_start = n)\n        core_nodes = nodes[iter * batch_size:end_idx]\n\n        start_time = time.perf_counter()\n        batch = neighbor_sampler.make_batch(core_nodes=core_nodes)\n        end_time = time.perf_counter()\n        process_times.append(end_time - start_time)\n\n        batch.split = split\n        batch.to(torch.device(cfg.accelerator))\n        if cfg.gnn.head == 'inductive_edge':\n            pred, true, extra_stats = model(batch)\n        else:\n            pred, true = model(batch)\n            extra_stats = {}\n\n        loss, pred_score = compute_loss(pred, true)\n        _true = true.detach().to('cpu', non_blocking=True)\n        _pred = pred_score.detach().to('cpu', non_blocking=True)\n        logger.update_stats(true=_true,\n                            pred=_pred,\n neighbor_sampler = NeighborSampler(loaders[0].dataset, edge_index, edge_attr, P, adj_eidx, \n                                       deg=cfg.train.edge_sample_num_neighbors, num_layers=cfg.gt.layers,\n     "Experiment Result": "Phase 1: Attention Score Estimator Network settings:\n- `cfg.gt.layer_type`: 'ASE' (defines the attention layer for phase 1)\n- `cfg.gt.n_heads`: typically 1 (narrow-width network, one attention head)\n- `cfg.gt.dim_hidden`: typically 64 (default hidden dimension)\n- `cfg.gt.dim_edge`: can be set to `cfg.gt.dim_hidden` if not specified (edge embedding dimension)\n- `cfg.gt.use_edge_feats`: boolean flag (uses learnable embeddings for edge types)\n- `cfg.train.temp_rdc_ratio`: temperature reduction ratio (e.g., 1.0, gradually reduced)\n- `cfg.train.temp_min`: minimum temperature (e.g., 0.05)\n- `cfg.train.temp_wait`: number of epochs to wait before starting temperature reduction\n- `cfg.train.save_attention_scores`: boolean flag to enable saving attention scores from Phase 1\n- `cfg.prep.exp_deg`: degree of the expander graph (e.g., 30-200)\n- `cfg.prep.exp_algorithm`: algorithm for expander graph generation ('Hamiltonian', 'Random-d', 'Random-d2')\n- `cfg.prep.exp_max_num_iters`: maximum iterations for expander graph generation\n- `cfg.dataset.edge_encoder_name`: 'TypeDictEdge' or 'TypeDictEdge2' (for learnable edge embeddings)\n- `cfg.dataset.edge_encoder_num_types`: number of edge types for encoding\n- `cfg.prep.add_self_loops`: boolean flag to add self-loops to the graph\n- `cfg.prep.add_reverse_edges`: boolean flag to add reverse edges for undirected graphs\n\nPhase 2: Sparsified Network Training settings:\n- `cfg.gt.layer_type`: 'Spexphormer', 'ExphormerFinal', 'ExphormerSecond', 'ExphormerRegularGraph' (defines the attention layer for phase 2)\n- `cfg.gt.n_heads`: number of attention heads (for the final, wider network)\n- `cfg.train.edge_sample_num_neighbors`: list of degrees `degℓ` for each layer (e.g., `[6, 4, 4, 4, 4, 4]`)\n- `cfg.train.spexphormer_sampler`: sampling strategy ('graph_reservoir', 'batch_reservoir', 'graph_max', 'graph_uniform', 'batch_uniform')\n- `cfg.train.resampling_epochs`: frequency (in epochs) of re-sampling edges\n- `cfg.optim.batch_accumulation`: number of batches to accumulate gradients over before updating parameters\n- `cfg.train.batch_size`: batch size for neighborhood expansion-based training\n- `cfg.train.mode`: 'custom_train' for the initial network (Phase 1), 'custom_with_sampling' for the final network (Phase 2)"

    "Title": "RSC: Accelerate Graph Neural Networks Training via Randomized Sparse Computations",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes Randomized Sparse Computation (RSC) to accelerate Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) training, addressing the time-consuming nature of sparse graph-based operations which are hard to accelerate on current hardware. RSC tackles two challenges unique to sparse operations: controlling approximation efficiency due to irregular data formats and reducing inefficient sparse matrix sampling. It achieves this by optimizing computation resource allocation layer-wise and epoch-wise, caching previously sampled sparse matrices, and switching back to original operations in the final training stage. RSC achieves up to 11.6x speedup for a single sparse operation and 1.6x end-to-end wall-clock time speedup with negligible accuracy drop (~0.3%).",
    "Methodology": "RSC uses a sampling-based approximation method, specifically top-k sampling, to replace expensive sparse operations, primarily in the backward pass of GNN training to ensure unbiased gradient calculation. To manage the accuracy-efficiency trade-off, it employs a layer-wise resource allocation strategy, formulated as a constrained optimization problem. This problem is solved using a greedy algorithm that determines the number of samples (k_l) for each layer by minimizing approximation error under a total FLOPs budget. An epoch-wise caching mechanism reuses previously sampled sparse matrices across nearby iterations to reduce sampling overhead. Finally, a switching mechanism is introduced where approximated operations are used for most of the training, reverting to original sparse operations in the final stage to improve convergence and minimize accuracy drop.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The effectiveness of RSC was evaluated on four large-scale graph benchmarks: Reddit, Yelp, ogbn-proteins, and ogbn-products. Experiments were conducted under both mini-batch training (integrating with GraphSAINT) and full-batch training settings (with GCN, GraphSAGE, and GCNII models). The hardware used for experiments included a server with four NVIDIA 3090 GPUs, AMD EPYC 7282 CPUs, and 252GB host memory. Evaluation metrics included wall-clock time speedup (T_baseline/T_rsc) and test accuracy/F1-micro/AUC, averaged over ten random trials. Hyperparameters like the overall budget C, greedy algorithm step size alpha, and the switching epoch percentage were studied.",
    "Limitations": "First, RSC only replaces sparse operations in the backward pass to guarantee model accuracy, which limits its maximum potential speedup. Second, the current framework does not cover GNNs that rely on scatter-and-gather operations (e.g., GAT) instead of SpMM (and its variants) for aggregation. While column-row sampling, caching, and switching mechanisms are applicable to scatter-and-gather operations, the resource allocation algorithm would require tailored error bounds and computation cost modeling for these operations.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work includes exploring RSC for GNNs that rely on scatter-and-gather operations, specifically by tailoring the error bounds and computation cost modeling in the resource allocation algorithm (Equation 4) to accommodate these operations.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null
