    "Title": "GATE: How to Keep Out Intrusive Neighbors",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper identifies and experimentally demonstrates a structural limitation of Graph Attention Networks (GATs): their inability to switch off task-irrelevant neighborhood aggregation. This limitation prevents GATs from fully realizing their potential, exacerbates over-smoothing, and hinders performance, particularly on heterophilic datasets. To address this, the authors propose GATE, an extension of GAT, which can flexibly switch neighborhood aggregation on and off as needed. GATE offers several advantages: it alleviates over-smoothing, benefits from higher model depth for non-linear feature transformations, often outperforms GATs on real-world heterophilic datasets by down-weighting unrelated neighbors, and provides interpretable learned self-attention coefficients. The work also constructs a synthetic test bed to validate claims and updates an existing conservation law related to GAT gradient dynamics for GATE, achieving a new state-of-the-art test accuracy on the OGB-arxiv dataset.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves modifying the GAT architecture based on theoretical insights into gradient flow dynamics. The original GAT is analyzed through a conservation law (Theorem 4.1) that reveals norm constraints on attention parameters, which restrict its ability to switch off neighborhood aggregation. GATE extends GAT by introducing separate attention parameters (as and at) for the node's own features and its neighborhood features, respectively. This modification allows GATE to independently control and flexibly weight the importance of self-features versus neighborhood features. A new conservation law (Theorem 4.3) is derived for GATE, demonstrating that it can switch off neighborhood aggregation in a well-trainable parameter regime (i.e., with relatively small attention parameter norms). The non-linearity for GATE is ReLU, contrasting with GAT's LeakyReLU, and feature transformation matrices are initialized with an orthogonal looks-linear structure, with GATE's attention parameters initialized to zero.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The validation of GATE's capabilities includes both synthetic and real-world graphs. The synthetic test bed comprises two node classification problems: 'self-sufficient learning' (label-relevant information only in node's own features, using Erdős–Rényi or Cora graph structures with one-hot encoded labels, either original or randomized) and 'neighbor-dependent learning' (label-relevant information in k-hop neighbors' features, using Erdős–Rényi graphs with multivariate normal features and K-means clustered labels). Real-world evaluations are performed on five heterophilic benchmark datasets (roman-empire, amazon-ratings, questions, minesweeper, tolokers), three OGB datasets (OGB-arxiv, OGB-products, OGB-mag), and five smaller-scale datasets (Cora, Citeseer, Actor, Texas, Wisconsin). Performance is measured using test accuracy and AUC-ROC. Baselines include GAT, MLP, MLP+GAT, FAGCN, GraphSAGE, ωGAT, and various other GNNs. Experiments are conducted for varying model depths (e.g., 5, 10, 12 layers). Optimization is performed using Adam, with learning rates adjusted per dataset, and for isolation of architectural effects, no weight decay or dropout regularization is typically used (except for specific comparisons). Over-smoothing is quantitatively assessed using a modified Dirichlet energy (EGAT, EGATE).",
    "Limitations": "The paper highlights GAT's fundamental limitation in effectively switching off task-irrelevant neighborhood aggregation due to gradient flow dynamics and norm constraints, a problem that GATE aims to solve. For the synthetic neighbor-dependent learning task, GATE cannot achieve perfect 100% test accuracy, which is attributed to data points near a not-crisply-defined decision boundary. When evaluating on smaller real-world datasets, the models (including GATE) are noted to be prone to overfitting in deeper configurations, especially without additional techniques like skip connections or regularization, which were sometimes omitted to isolate the architectural effects. The empirical failure of FAGCN to switch off neighborhood aggregation, even when theoretically capable, is also noted. The paper acknowledges that the concept of 'over-smoothing' is task-dependent and difficult to precisely define or measure with an optimal threshold.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The authors suggest that GATE is a suitable candidate for fundamental research into the importance of graph structure for various tasks. Specific future research directions include deriving conservation laws for other GNN architectures, such as FAGCN and GraphSAGE, to understand their parameter behaviors. Additionally, combining GATE's strengths with complementary techniques, such as FAGCN's capacity to learn negative associations with neighboring nodes, is identified as a promising avenue for further development.",
    "Experiment Code": null,

    "Title": "Bandit Samplers for Training Graph Neural Networks",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the challenge of accelerating Graph Neural Network (GNN) training by optimizing sampling variance. Existing sampling algorithms for Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs) are suboptimal and not applicable to more general GNNs (e.g., Graph Attention Networks) due to the intractable computation of optimal sampling distributions involving dynamic node embeddings and learned weights. The authors reformulate the optimization of sampling variance as an adversary bandit problem, where rewards are related to changing node embeddings and learned weights. This approach allows a sampler to balance exploration (acquiring variance information) and exploitation (optimizing immediate sampling variance). The key contributions include: (1) recasting GNN samplers as a bandit problem applicable to both GCNs and attentive GNNs, (2) theoretically proving that the regret of their estimators asymptotically approximates the optimal variance within a factor of 3, and (3) empirically demonstrating superior convergence rates, better accuracy, and lower sample variances compared to state-of-the-art approaches on multiple datasets.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves formulating the optimization of sampling variance as an adversary bandit problem. Instead of computing an intractable optimal sampling distribution at each iteration, the approach maintains nonparametric estimates of the sampler and updates it iteratively based on partial knowledge from sampled neighbors. The reward for choosing a set of neighbors is defined as the negative derivative of the sampling variance. Two bandit algorithms are proposed: (1) GNN-BS, based on a multi-armed bandit (MAB) setting, where a single neighbor is sampled and repeated 'k' times, with updates done using EXP3. (2) GNN-BS.M, based on MAB with multiple plays, which uses an efficient k-combination sampler (DepRound) to select a combinatorial set of 'k' neighbors at once, with updates done using EXP3.M. For attentive GNNs, the algorithms are extended by defining adjusted feedback attention values to approximate true attention values, as unnormalized attentions are the only observable values during sampling. The effective variance for GNN-BS is derived directly, while for GNN-BS.M, an approximated effective variance is used based on Jensen's inequality.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experimental evaluation was conducted on five benchmark datasets: Cora, Pubmed, PPI, Reddit, and Flickr, using standard data splits. The GNN architectures used for comparison were GCN, GAT, and GeniePath, with a fixed two-layer structure and varying hidden embedding dimensions (16 for Cora/Pubmed, 256 for others). A normalization layer was intentionally omitted for a fair comparison. Hyperparameters like learning rate, L2 regularization, and dropout rate were optimized via grid search. Sample sizes ('k') for GraphSAGE, S-GCN, and the proposed algorithms were set to 1 for Cora/Pubmed, 5 for Flickr, and 10 for PPI/Reddit, with specific layer-dependent sample sizes for other methods like FastGCN and AS-GCN. Batch size was 256 for layer sampling approaches and S-GCN. ClusterGCN and GraphSAINT used their recommended configurations. The performance was measured using Micro F1 scores on testing data, convergence rates on validation data (in terms of epochs and seconds), and analysis of sampling variances as sample size varies. Experiments were run 3 times for main results and 10 times for OGB protein dataset, reporting mean and standard deviation. The proposed GP-BS algorithm was also evaluated on the OGB protein dataset.",
    "Limitations": "The paper acknowledges a limitation regarding the strict applicability of the GNN-BS algorithm: in the adversary MAB setting, it samples 'k' times and then updates the policy, which is not strictly rigorous compared to the bandit setting where one action is followed by an update. However, empirical results showed its performance to be very similar to the more rigorous GNN-BS.M. Another limitation is that the derivation of the bandit samplers primarily follows a node-wise sampling approach, and its extension to layer-wise sampling is left as future work. The paper also points out that while 'graph sampling' approaches might be faster under certain conditions, they are generally less flexible and not applicable to scenarios where only partial vertices have labels, making 'layer sampling' approaches (like the one proposed) more general.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The authors propose two main directions for future research. First, extending the bandit samplers, which are currently derived for node-wise sampling, to accommodate layer-wise sampling approaches. Second, exploring the application and efficacy of their framework under other bandit settings, moving beyond the adversary bandit setting investigated in this paper.",

(attention_mechanism function from models.py, used for feedback) ...def attention_mechanism(name, v, W_s, W_d, V, cur_embed, left, right, n2n):    # a_{i,j} \\propto v^\\top tanh (W_s (\\mu_i + \\mu_j))    if name == 'linear':        t = tf.sparse_tensor_dense_matmul(sp_a=edge, b=cur_embed) # edge \\in \\R^{m, n}        t = tf.matmul(t, W_s) # m by 16        t = tf.nn.tanh(t)        t = tf.matmul(t, tf.reshape(v, [-1,1])) # m by 1        sparse_attention = tf.SparseTensor(n2n.indices, tf.reshape(t, [-1]), n2n.dense_shape)        sparse_attention = tf.sparse_softmax(sparse_attention)    # a_{i,j} \\propto v^\\top tanh (W_s |\\mu_i - \\mu_j|)    elif name == 'abs':        t = tf.sparse_tensor_dense_matmul(sp_a=edge, b=cur_embed) # edge \\in \\R^{m, n}        t = tf.abs(t)        t = tf.matmul(t, W_s) # m by 16        t = tf.nn.tanh(t)        t = tf.matmul(t, tf.reshape(v, [-1,1])) # m by 1        sparse_attention = tf.SparseTensor(n2n.indices, tf.reshape(t, [-1]), n2n.dense_shape)        sparse_attention = tf.sparse_softmax(sparse_attention)    # a_{i,j} \\propto leakyrelu (\\mu_i V \\mu_j)    elif name == 'bilinear':        tl = tf.sparse_tensor_dense_matmul(sp_a=left, b=cur_embed) # m by k        tl = tf.matmul(tl, V)        tr = tf.sparse_tensor_dense_matmul(sp_a=right, b=cur_embed)        tr = tf.matmul(tr, V)        t = tf.reduce_sum(tf.multiply(tl, tr), 1, keep_dims=True)        t = tf.keras.layers.LeakyReLU(t)        sparse_attention = tf.SparseTensor(n2n.indices, tf.reshape(t, [-1]), n2n.dense_shape)        sparse_attention = tf.sparse_softmax(sparse_attention)    # a_{i,j} \\propto v^\\top tanh (W_s \\mu_i + W_d \\mu_j)    if name == 'generalized_linear':        tl = tf.sparse_tensor_dense_matmul(sp_a=left, b=cur_embed) # m by k        tl = tf.matmul(tl, W_s)        tr = tf.sparse_tensor_dense_matmul(sp_a=right, b=cur_embed)        tr = tf.matmul(tr, W_d)        t = tf.nn.tanh(tf.add(tl,tr))        t = tf.matmul(t, tf.reshape(v, [-1,1]))        sparse_attention = tf.SparseTensor(n2n.indices, tf.reshape(t, [-1]), n2n.dense_shape)        sparse_attention = tf.sparse_softmax(sparse_attention)    else:        sys.exit(-1)    return sparse_attention# ...     "Title": "Are GATs Out of Balance?",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper investigates the optimization and learning dynamics of Graph Attention Networks (GATs). It derives a conservation law for GAT gradient flow dynamics, explaining why a significant portion of parameters in GATs with standard initialization struggle to change during training, a problem amplified in deeper networks. To address this, the paper proposes a novel balanced initialization scheme that enables more effective gradient propagation, facilitates the trainability of deeper GATs, and achieves considerable speedup in training and convergence time. This work also serves as a foundational step for studying the learning dynamics of positive homogeneous models incorporating attention mechanisms.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves deriving a conservation law for GATs with positive homogeneous activation functions (e.g., ReLU, LeakyReLU). This law reveals a structural relationship between model parameters and their gradients, specifically that the difference between the squared L2-norms of incoming and outgoing parameters to a neuron, adjusted for attention parameters, remains approximately constant under gradient flow. Based on this insight, the paper proposes a 'balanced initialization' procedure. This scheme involves setting attention parameters to zero and scaling feature transformation weights (Wl) such that the norm preservation condition (Wl[i, :]^2 - al[i]^2 = Wl+1[: i]^2) is met layer-wise. An advanced variant, Balanced Orthogonal Initialization (BalO), incorporates a 'looks-linear mirrored block structure' orthogonal initialization for feature weights, aiming to enhance dynamical isometry before applying the balancing procedure.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on GATv2 models, primarily using ReLU activation and weight sharing (also testing ELU, multiple attention heads, no weight sharing, dropout, and weight decay). Four main initialization schemes were evaluated: standard Xavier (Xav), Xavier with zero attention (XavZ), balanced Xavier (BalX), and balanced LL-orthogonal (BalO), alongside other orthogonal variations like identity and LLidentity. Nine benchmark datasets for semi-supervised node classification were used: Cora, Citeseer, Pubmed, Actor, Chameleon, Cornell, Squirrel, Texas, and Wisconsin, utilizing standard train/validation/test splits. Models were trained for up to 5000 epochs with SGD or Adam optimizers, with learning rates adapted for different depths and datasets. Performance was measured by mean test accuracy (%) and epochs to the best model (95% confidence interval over five runs). The research also included visualizations of parameter relative change and relative gradient norms, and comparisons against the LipschitzNorm technique for deep GNNs.",
    "Limitations": "The derived conservation law specifically applies to the self-attention mechanism found in the original GAT and GATv2 models, and architectural variations like ωGAT. It does not directly apply to other types of self-attention, such as dot-product self-attention mechanisms, which would require modifications to the law. Additionally, the theoretical assumptions of positive homogeneity for activation functions mean that using non-homogeneous functions like ELU can negatively impact the effectiveness of the balanced orthogonal initialization, although Adam optimization may partially compensate. Practical observations showed that techniques like dropout are not universally beneficial for deeper networks, and while orthogonal initialization aids trainability, it doesn't achieve perfect dynamical isometry in GNNs due to neighborhood aggregation. Furthermore, identity initialization, though sometimes effective, may present theoretical challenges regarding feature diversity.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research could extend the study of learning dynamics to other positive homogeneous models that incorporate attention mechanisms, such as various transformer architectures used in sequence models, large language models (LLMs), and vision transformers. Specifically, adapting and modifying the derived conservation law for different self-attention mechanisms, like dot-product self-attention in models such as SuperGAT, is an intriguing direction. Further exploration into how to achieve or approximate perfect dynamical isometry in general Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) is also suggested. Finally, the observed phenomenon where increased network width aids generalization performance in deeper GNNs, even with imbalanced initializations, warrants independent investigation into the helpfulness of overparameterization in GNNs.",
    "Experiment Code": "The method describes a 'balanced initialization' procedure involving setting attention parameters to zero and scaling feature transformation weights (Wl) to meet a norm preservation condition, and an advanced 'Balanced Orthogonal Initialization (BalO)' for feature weights. However, the provided `layers.py` code for `GraphAttentionLayer` and `SpGraphAttentionLayer` does not implement these custom initialization schemes. Instead, both the feature transformation weights (`self.W`) and attention mechanism parameters (`self.a`) are initialized using standard Xavier initialization methods: `nn.init.xavier_uniform_` or `nn.init.xavier_normal_` with a `gain=1.414`. No explicit code for 'setting attention parameters to zero' or custom 'scaling feature transformation weights' based on norm conditions, nor for 'looks-linear mirrored block structure' orthogonal initialization, is present in the provided repository content.",
    "Experiment Result": "The experiments are conducted on the Cora citation network dataset, loaded using `utils.load_data()`. The model used is a Graph Attention Network (GAT) or its sparse version (SpGAT), configured with specific hyperparameters.     "Title": "Are GATs Out of Balance?",
    "Main Contributions": "The research identifies a major trainability issue in Graph Attention Networks (GATs) with standard initialization, where a high portion of parameters struggle to change, especially in deeper networks. It derives a conservation law of GAT gradient flow dynamics to explain this phenomenon. To alleviate the problem, the paper proposes a balanced initialization scheme that allows more effective gradient propagation, enables the trainability of deeper GATs, and achieves considerable speedup in training and convergence time. This work also serves as a stepping stone for studying the learning dynamics of positive homogeneous models with attention mechanisms.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves deriving a conservation law for GAT gradient flow dynamics, including variations like shared feature weights and multiple attention heads, using the concept of neuron-wise balancedness from traditional deep neural networks. This law explains why parameter norms become unbalanced during training with standard initialization. Based on this insight, a balanced initialization scheme is proposed (Procedure 2.6). This scheme sets attention parameters to zero and scales feature weights to ensure the L2-norms of incoming and outgoing weights to a neuron are balanced (c=0). Specifically, it scales Wl+1[:, i] based on Wl[i, :] norms. Two balanced initialization variants are tested: BalX (Balanced Xavier) and BalO (Balanced Looks-Linear Orthogonal initialization, where feature weights are initially orthogonal matrices with a looks-linear mirrored block structure).",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on nine benchmark datasets for semi-supervised node classification: Cora, Citeseer, Pubmed, Actor, Chameleon, Cornell, Squirrel, Texas, and Wisconsin. Training was performed for 5000 epochs (or until training loss <= 10^-4) with ReLU activation, weight sharing, and no biases (unless specified), using SGD and Adam optimizers. Learning rates were fine-tuned per depth and dataset. Evaluation metrics included mean test accuracy (%) and epochs to the best model, reported with 95% confidence intervals over five runs. The study also visualized the fraction of significant parameters with relative change and relative gradient norms across layers.",
    "Limitations": "The derived conservation law specifically applies to the self-attention mechanisms defined in the original GAT and GATv2 models, and their variations like ωGAT. Modifications to the conservation law would be required for other forms of self-attention, such as dot-product self-attention found in models like SuperGAT or Transformers. While some architectural variations (e.g., ELU activation, dropout, weight decay) were explored, ELU is not a positively homogeneous function, which impacts the theoretical assumptions, particularly for the BalO initialization. The study does not compare with state-of-the-art specialized models on heterophilic datasets, as its primary aim is to highlight an underlying mechanism for trainability rather than to achieve peak performance.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include extending the study of learning dynamics to other positive homogeneous models that incorporate attention mechanisms, such as Transformers and Vision Transformers, given their increased need for depth.     "Title": "GraphSAINT: Graph Sampling Based Inductive Learning Method",
    "Main Contributions": "GraphSAINT (Graph SAmpling based INductive learning meThod) addresses the 'neighbor explosion' problem in Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs) when scaling to large graphs and deep models. It fundamentally changes minibatch construction by sampling the training graph first, then building a complete GCN on the sampled subgraph, ensuring a fixed number of well-connected nodes in all layers. Key contributions include normalization techniques to eliminate bias from non-identical node sampling, variance reduction-inspired sampling algorithms, and flexibility to integrate with various GCN architectures (e.g., Graph Attention Networks, Jumping Knowledge Networks). GraphSAINT demonstrates superior accuracy and training time on five large datasets, achieving new state-of-the-art F1 scores for PPI (0.995) and Reddit (0.970).",
    "Methodology": "GraphSAINT's core methodology involves constructing minibatches by directly sampling subgraphs from the training graph, rather than sampling nodes or edges across GCN layers. This resolves neighbor explosion because a full GCN is then built on the small, complete subgraph. To address bias introduced by non-identical node/edge sampling probabilities, it proposes aggregator normalization (αu,v = pu,v / pv) for feature propagation and loss normalization (λv = |V| * pv) for minibatch loss. These probabilities are estimated via pre-processing by repeatedly running the sampler. For variance reduction, optimal edge probabilities (pe ∝ |∑ℓ b(ℓ)e|) are derived, simplifying to pe ∝ 1/deg(u) + 1/deg(v) based on graph topology. The paper details several light-weight samplers: Random Node Sampler (P(u) ∝ ||˜A:,u||^2), Random Edge Sampler (P((u,v)) ∝ 1/deg(u) + 1/deg(v)), and Random Walk-based Samplers (regular Random Walk and Multi-dimensional Random Walk), all returning node-induced subgraphs. The method decouples sampling from forward/backward propagation, allowing seamless integration with architectures like JK-net and GAT.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments are conducted under an inductive, supervised learning setting for node classification tasks on five large, real-world attributed graphs: PPI (protein functions), Flickr (image categories), Reddit (online post communities), Yelp (business categories), and Amazon (product categories). Dataset sizes range from 14,755 to 1,598,960 nodes and from 225,270 to 132,169,734 edges. All datasets use 'fixed-partition' splits for training, validation, and testing. GraphSAINT is compared against six state-of-the-art baselines: vanilla GCN, GraphSAGE, FastGCN, S-GCN, AS-GCN, and ClusterGCN. Evaluation is based on the F1-micro score on the test set. The hardware setup includes a single machine with Dual Intel Xeon CPUs, an NVIDIA Tesla P100 GPU (16GB HBM2), and 512GB DDR4 memory. Experiments utilize Python 3.6.8, Cython 0.29.2 for sampling, and TensorFlow 1.12.0 on CUDA 9.2. Adam optimizer is used, and hyperparameters (hidden dimension, dropout, learning rate) are optimized via grid search.",
    "Limitations": "One practical limitation is the complexity of exactly calculating the optimal edge probabilities for variance reduction, as it requires computing feature representations from previous layers (˜x(ℓ-1)v). This leads to a reasonable simplification where edge probabilities are made dependent only on graph topology (e.g., inverse degree sums). Additionally, analyzing random walk-based samplers by representing L layers as ˜A^L ignores non-linear activations, which is a simplification in the theoretical variance analysis. Explicit variance reduction for Graph Attention Networks (GAT) is challenging due to dynamically updated attention values, though the proposed normalization techniques are still applicable.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include developing distributed training algorithms for GraphSAINT. This would involve partitioning the training graph across distributed memory, allowing independent sampling on each processor, and training on self-supportive subgraphs to significantly reduce system-level communication. Ensuring overall convergence quality would require developing data shuffling strategies integrated with specific graph samplers. Another direction is to perform algorithm-system co-optimization to accelerate GraphSAINT training on heterogeneous computing platforms (e.g., CPU-FPGA). This would capitalize on the reduced computational complexity and improved hardware utilization gained from resolving the 'neighbor explosion' problem, and leverage the decoupled nature of sampling and GCN propagation for easier task-level parallelization.",
    "Experiment Code": "class GraphSampler:    def __init__(self, adj_train, node_train, size_subgraph, args_preproc):        self.adj_train = adj_train        self.node_train = np.unique(node_ids)        node_ids.sort()        orig2subg = {n: i for i, n in enumerate(node_ids)}        n = node_ids.size        indptr = np.zeros(node_ids.size + 1)        indices = []        subg_edge_index = []        subg_nodes = node_ids        for nid in node_ids:            idx_s, idx_e = self.adj_train.indptr[nid], self.adj_train.indptr[nid + 1]            neighs = self.adj_train.indices[idx_s : idx_e]            for i_n, n in enumerate(neighs):                if n in orig2subg:                    indices.append(orig2subg[n])                    indptr[orig2subg[nid] + 1] += 1                    subg_edge_index.append(idx_s + i_n)        indptr = indptr.cumsum().astype(np.int64)        indices = np.array(indices)        subg_edge_index = self.adj_train.indices,                self.adj_train.indptr            ),            shape=self.adj_train.shape,        )        self.edge_prob.data[:] = self.adj_train_norm.data[:]        _adj_trans = scipy.sparse.csr_matrix.tocsc(self.adj_train_norm)        self.edge_prob.data += _adj_trans.data        self.edge_prob.data *= 2 * self.num_edges_subgraph / self.edge_prob.data.sum() = 0        while True:            self.par_graph_sample('train')            tot_sampled_nodes = sum([len(n) for n in self.subgraphs_remaining_nodes])            if tot_sampled_nodes > self.sample_coverage * self.node_train.size:                break        num_subg = len(self.subgraphs_remaining_nodes)        for i in range(num_subg): aggr='mean', bias='norm-nn', **kwargs):        super(HighOrderAggregator,self).__init__()        assert bias in ['bias', 'norm', 'norm-nn']        self.order, self.aggr = order, aggr        self.act, self.bias = F_ACT[act], bias        self.dropout = dropout        self.f_lin, self.f_bias = [], []        self.offset, self.scale = [], []        self.num_param = 0        for o in range(self.order + 1):            self.f_lin.append(nn.Linear(dim_in, dim_out, bias=False))            nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.f_lin[-1].weight)            self.f_bias.append(nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(dim_out)))            self.num_param += dim_in * dim_out            self.num_param += dim_out            self.offset.append(nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(dim_out)))            self.scale.append(nn.Parameter(torch.ones(dim_out)))            if self.bias == 'norm' or self.bias == 'norm-nn':                self.num_param += 2 * dim_out        self.f_lin = nn.ModuleList(self.f_lin)        self.f_dropout = nn.Dropout(p=self.dropout)        self.params = nn.ParameterList(self.f_bias + self.offset + self.scale)        self.f_bias = self.params[:self.order + 1]        if self.bias == 'norm':            self.offset = self.params[self.order + 1 : 2 * self.order + 2]            self.scale = self.params[2 * self.order + 2 : ]        elif self.bias == 'norm-nn':            final_dim_out = dim_out * ((aggr=='concat') * (order + 1) + (aggr=='mean'))            self.f_norm = nn.BatchNorm1d(final_dim_out, eps=1e-9, track_running_stats=True)        self.num_param = int(self.num_param)    def _spmm(self, adj_norm, _feat):        return torch.sparse.mm(adj_norm, _feat)    def _f_feat_trans(self, _feat, _id):        feat = self.act(self.f_lin[_id](_feat) + self.f_bias[_id])        if self.bias == 'norm':            mean = feat.mean(dim=1).view(feat.shape[0],1)            var = feat.var(dim=1, unbiased=False).view(feat.shape[0], 1) + 1e-9            feat_out = (feat - mean) * self.scale[_id] * torch.rsqrt(var) + self.offset[_id]        else:            feat_out = feat        return feat_out    def forward(self, inputs):        adj_norm, feat_in = inputs        feat_in = self.f_dropout(feat_in)        feat_hop = [feat_in]        for o in range(self.order):            feat_hop.append(self._spmm(adj_norm, feat_hop[-1]))        feat_partial = [self._f_feat_trans(ft, idf) for idf, ft in enumerate(feat_hop)]        if self.aggr == 'mean':            feat_out = feat_partial[0]            for o in range(len(feat_partial) - 1):                feat_out += feat_partial[o + 1]        elif self.aggr == 'concat':            feat_out = torch.cat(feat_partial, 1) __init__(self, dim_in, dim_out, dropout=0., act='relu',             order=1, aggr='mean', bias='norm', mulhead=1):        super(AttentionAggregator,self).__init__()        assert bias in ['bias', 'norm', 'norm-nn']        self.num_param = 0        self.mulhead = mulhead        self.order, self.aggr = order, aggr        self.act, self.bias = F_ACT[act], bias        self.att_act = nn.LeakyReLU(negative_slope=0.2)        self.dropout = dropout        self._f_lin = []        self._offset, self._scale = [], []        self._attention = []        for o in range(self.order+1):            for i in range(self.mulhead):                self._f_lin.append(nn.Linear(dim_in, int(dim_out / self.mulhead), bias=True))                nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self._f_lin[-1].weight)                self._offset.append(nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(int(dim_out / self.mulhead))))                self._scale.append(nn.Parameter(torch.ones(int(dim_out / self.mulhead))))                self.num_param += dim_in * dim_out / self.mulhead + 2 * dim_out / self.mulhead                if o < self.order:                    self._attention.append(nn.Parameter(torch.ones(1, int(dim_out / self.mulhead * 2))))                    nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self._attention[-1])                    self.num_param += dim_out / self.mulhead * 2        self.mods = nn.ModuleList(self._f_lin) self.attention = []        for o in range(self.order+1):            self.f_lin.append([])            self.offset.append([])            self.scale.append([])            self.attention.append([])            for i in range(self.mulhead):                self.f_lin[-1].append(self.mods[o * self.mulhead + i])                if self.bias == 'norm':                    self.offset[-1].append(self.params[o * self.mulhead + i])                    self.scale[-1].append(self.params[len(self._offset) + o * self.mulhead + i])                if o < self.order:           self.f_norm = nn.BatchNorm1d(final_dim_out, eps=1e-9, track_running_stats=True)        self.num_param = int(self.num_param)    def _spmm(self, adj_norm, _feat):        return torch.sparse.mm(adj_norm, _feat)    def _f_feat_trans(self, _feat, f_lin):        feat_out = []        for i in range(self.mulhead):            feat_out.append(self.act(f_lin[i](_feat)))        return feat_out    def _aggregate_attention(self, adj, feat_neigh, feat_self, attention):        attention_self = self.att_act(attention[:, : feat_self.shape[1]].mm(feat_self.t())).squeeze()        attention_neigh = self.att_act(attention[:, feat_neigh.shape[1] :].mm(feat_neigh.t())).squeeze()        attention_norm = (attention_self[adj._indices()[0]] + attention_neigh[adj._indices()[1]]) * adj._values()        att_adj = torch.sparse.FloatTensor(adj._indices(), attention_norm, torch.Size(adj.shape))        return self._spmm(att_adj, feat_neigh)    def forward(self, inputs):        adj_norm, feat_in = inputs        feat_in = self.f_dropout(feat_in)        feat_partial = []        for o in range(self.order + 1):            feat_partial.append(self._f_feat_trans(feat_in, self.f_lin[o]))        for o in range(1,self.order + 1):            for s in range(o):                for i in range(self.mulhead):                    feat_partial[o][i] = self._aggregate_attention(                        adj_norm,                        feat_partial[o][i],                        feat_partial[o - s - 1][i],                        self.attention[o-1][i],                    ) var = feat_partial[o][i].var(dim=1, unbiased=False).unsqueeze(1) + 1e-9                    feat_partial[o][i] = (feat_partial[o][i] - mean)                                 * self.scale[o][i] * torch.rsqrt(var) + self.offset[o][i]        for o in range(self.order + 1):            feat_partial[o] = torch.cat(feat_partial[o], 1)        if self.aggr == 'mean':            feat_out = feat_partial[0]            for o in range(len(feat_partial) - 1):                feat_out += feat_partial[o + 1]        elif self.aggr == 'concat':            feat_out = torch.cat(feat_partial, 1)     "Experiment Result": "Experimental settings for GraphSAINT are configured via command-line arguments and a YAML training configuration file.Key parameters and settings include:1.  **Hardware & Parallelism**:    *   `--num_cpu_core` (default: 20): Number of CPU cores for parallel sampling.    *   `--gpu` (default: auto-detected, e.g., '0'): Specifies which GPU to use, with automatic detection logic to find an available GPU.    *   `os.environ[\"CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES\"]`: Set based on GPU selection.    *   `GPU_MEM_FRACTION`: Set to 0.8 when a specific GPU is used.    *   `SAMPLES_PER_PROC`: Derived from `num_cpu_core` (round up division of 200 by `num_cpu_core`).    *   TensorFlow session configuration includes `device_count`, `inter_op_parallelism_threads`, `intra_op_parallelism_threads`, and `log_device_placement`.2.  **Data & Logging**:    *   `--data_prefix` (required): Prefix identifying training data files (e.g., `adj_full.npz`, `feats.npy`).    *   `--dir_log` (default: \".\"): Base directory for logging and saving embeddings.    *   `--timeline`: Flag to save `timeline.json` for profiling.    *   `--tensorboard`: Flag to save data to TensorBoard.3.  **Model Architecture (`arch_gcn` loaded from YAML config)**:    *   `dim`: Output dimension for GNN layers.    *   `aggr`: Aggregation method ('concat' or 'mean').    *   `loss`: Loss function type ('softmax' or 'sigmoid').    *   `arch`: String defining layer orders (e.g., '1-0-1' for a 3-layer GNN with hop orders 1, 0, 1).    *   `act`: Activation function ('relu' or 'I' for identity).    *   `bias`: Bias/normalization method ('norm', 'norm-nn', or 'bias').    *   `attention`: Integer indicating number of attention heads for GAT-like aggregators.    *   `gated_attention`: Boolean to enable Gated Attention Aggregator.    *   `jk`: Jumping Knowledge network mode ('concat', 'max_pool', or None).4.  **Training Parameters (`train_params` loaded from YAML config)**:    *   `lr` (default: 0.01): Learning rate for the Adam optimizer.    *   `weight_decay` (default: 0.): L2 regularization coefficient.    *   `dropout`: Dropout rate (set by argument `--dropout`).    *   `norm_loss`: Boolean, whether to apply loss normalization.    *   `norm_aggr`: Boolean, whether to apply aggregator normalization.    *   `q_threshold`, `q_offset`: Parameters for quantization (details not in provided code snippets).5.  **Sampling Parameters (`train_phases` loaded from YAML config)**:    *   `sampler`: Sampling method ('mrw', 'rw', 'edge', 'node', 'full_batch', 'vanilla_node_python').    *   `size_subgraph`: Target number of nodes in a sampled subgraph (for 'node' and 'mrw' samplers).    *   `num_root`: Number of root nodes for random walk samplers.    *   `depth`: Length of random walks.    *   `size_subg_edge`: Number of edges to sample for edge-based sampler.    *   `size_frontier`: Size of the frontier for Multi-dimensional Random Walk.    *   `deg_clip`: Degree clipping for MRW sampler (default: 100000).    *   `sample_coverage`: A ratio used in `Minibatch.set_sampler` to determine how many total sampled nodes are needed before stopping the warm-up phase for normalization estimation (e.g., `tot_sampled_nodes > self.sample_coverage * self.node_train.size`).6.  **Evaluation Settings**:    *   `--eval_train_every` (default: 15): How often to evaluate training subgraph accuracy.    *   `EVAL_VAL_EVERY_EP` (default: 1): How often to evaluate validation set accuracy (per epoch).    *   `--cpu_eval`: Flag to perform full-batch evaluation on CPU.    *   Metrics: Micro F1 score and Macro F1 score (calculated by `graphsaint.metric.calc_f1`). For OGB datasets, `ogb.nodeproppred.Evaluator` with 'acc' is used for evaluation.    *   `--saved_model_path`: Path to a pretrained model file.7.  **Data Type**:    *   `--dtype` (default: 's' for single precision): 's' for float32, 'd' for float64. This sets the global `DTYPE` variable."
}{
    "Title": "Bandit Samplers for Training Graph Neural Networks",
    "Main Contributions": "This paper addresses the challenge of suboptimal and inapplicable sampling algorithms for training general Graph Neural Networks (GNNs), particularly those with learned weights like Graph Attention Networks (GAT). Existing methods struggle because the optimal sampling distribution depends on constantly changing node embeddings and learned weights, which are not known a priori. The authors reformulate the optimization of sampling variance as an adversary bandit problem. They propose two bandit algorithms, GNN-BS (based on Multi-Armed Bandit) and GNN-BS.M (based on MAB with multiple plays), which maintain nonparametric estimates of the sampler and update them based on partial knowledge. Theoretically, their algorithms asymptotically approach the optimal variance within a factor of 3. Empirically, their approach demonstrates superior convergence, faster training rates, and lower sample variances on multiple datasets compared to state-of-the-art methods, working effectively for both GCNs and attentive GNNs.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves formulating the sampling variance optimization as an adversary bandit problem, where rewards are related to node embeddings and learned weights that vary during training. Instead of directly computing an intractable optimal sampling distribution, the proposed algorithms maintain nonparametric estimates of the sampler. Two specific bandit algorithms are introduced: GNN-BS and GNN-BS.M. GNN-BS operates in an adversary Multi-Armed Bandit (MAB) setting, where it samples one neighbor and repeats this k times, updating the sampler using the EXP3 algorithm. The reward for picking a neighbor is defined as the negative derivative of the effective variance, proportional to (alpha_ij^2 / q_ij^2) * ||h_j||^2. GNN-BS.M is designed for the adversary MAB with multiple plays setting, using the DepRound sampler to select a k-element subset of neighbors once. It uses an approximated effective variance (derived via Jensen's inequality) and updates the sampler using EXP3.M. The reward for selecting a subset of neighbors is derived as the sum of (alpha_ij / q_ij^2) * ||h_j||^2 for neighbors in the sampled subset. For attentive GNNs, where attention values (alpha_ij) are learned and cannot be fully evaluated with sampled neighborhoods, the algorithms use 'adjusted feedback attention values' (alpha'_ij) as an approximation based on sampled unnormalized attentions. The theoretical analysis demonstrates that the regret with respect to the variance of their estimators asymptotically approximates the optimal sampler within a factor of 3.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experimental evaluation was conducted on five benchmark datasets: Cora, Pubmed, PPI, Reddit, and Flickr, along with an additional test on the OGB protein dataset. The primary GNN architectures used for comparison were GCN, GAT, and GeniePath (GP). Comparison methods included various 'layer sampling' approaches (GraphSAGE, FastGCN, LADIES, AS-GCN, S-GCN) and 'graph sampling' techniques (ClusterGCN, GraphSAINT). For attentive GNNs, AS-GAT and GraphSAINT-GAT were used as baselines. All GNN architectures were configured with 2 layers, and hidden embedding dimensions were set to 16 for Cora/Pubmed, 256 for PPI/Reddit/Flickr, and 64 for OGB protein. No normalization layer was used, and attentive GNNs used 1 multi-head for simplicity. Standard data splits were followed for all datasets. Hyperparameters, including learning rate, L2 regularization, and dropout rate, were determined via grid search, and models were selected based on the best performance on validation data. Sample sizes (k) for GraphSAGE, S-GCN, and the proposed algorithms were set to 1 for Cora/Pubmed, 5 for Flickr, and 10 for PPI/Reddit. Batch size for layer sampling and S-GCN was 256. ClusterGCN and GraphSAINT parameters were set following their original papers or through grid search. Performance was primarily measured by Micro F1 scores, and convergence rates (epochs and time) and sample variances were also analyzed. Experiments were run 3 times (10 times for OGB protein) to report mean and standard deviation.",
    "Limitations": "The GNN-BS algorithm, based on the adversary Multi-Armed Bandit setting, samples neighbors k times repeatedly. This is acknowledged as 'not rigorous' in a strict bandit setting, where typically one action is made per update. The authors maintain a single `qi` (alternative sampling distribution) for all layers, updating it only using rewards from the first layer, which is an approximation despite being found to work well in practice. The initial derivation of the bandit samplers follows node-wise sampling approaches, and extending this to layer-wise sampling is left as future work. For GNN-BS.M, the effective variance is approximated using Jensen's inequality rather than being an exact calculation. The paper explicitly avoids comparing the timing with 'graph sampling' approaches due to their differing applicability (graph sampling often assumes all vertices have labels, which is not always the case in practice), thus limiting the scope of efficiency comparisons.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The authors suggest exploring other bandit settings beyond the adversary bandit setting adopted in this paper. Another explicit future direction is to extend the derivation of their bandit samplers from node-wise sampling approaches to layer-wise sampling approaches. Implicitly, further work could involve a more comprehensive comparison with graph sampling approaches under conditions where only partial vertices have labels, given their discussion on the limitations of graph sampling in such scenarios.",
    "Experiment Code": "from utils import *\nimport scipy.sparse as sp\nfrom sparse_tensor_utils import *\n\n\nclass Sampler(object):\n    \"\"\"Sampling and Network Constructing\"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(self, placeholders, **kwargs):\n        allowed_kwargs = {'num_layers', 'input_dim', 'layer_sizes', 'scope'}\n        for kwarg in kwargs.keys():\n            assert kwarg in allowed_kwargs, 'Invalid keyword argument: ' + kwarg\n\n        self.input_dim = kwargs.get('input_dim', 1)\n        self.layer_sizes = kwargs.get('layer_sizes', [1])\n        self.scope = kwargs.get('scope', 'test_graph')\n\n        self.num_layers = len(self.layer_sizes)\n\n        with tf.variable_scope(self.scope, reuse=True):\n            self.w_s = tf.get_variable('sample_weights', [self.input_dim, 2])\n\n        self.adj = tf.Variable(placeholders['adj'], trainable=False, name=\"adj\")\n        self.adj_val = tf.Variable(placeholders['adj_val'], trainable=False, name=\"adj_val\")\n        self.x = tf.Variable(placeholders['features'], trainable=False, name=\"x\")\n\n        self.num_data = tf.shape(self.adj)[0]\n\n    def sampling(self, v):\n        pass\n\n\nclass SamplerAdapt(Sampler):\n    \"\"\"Parameters of the sampler are adaptive\"\"\"\n\n    def sampling(self, v):\n        all_support = [[]] * (self.num_layers - 1)\n        all_p_u = [[]] * (self.num_layers - 1)\n        all_x_u = [[]] * self.num_layers\n\n        # sample top-1 layer\n        this_x_v = tf.gather(self.x, v)\n        this_adj = tf.gather(self.adj, v)\n        this_adj_val = tf.gather(self.adj_val, v)\n        all_x_u[self.num_layers - 1] = this_x_v\n\n        # top-down sampling from top-2 layer to the input layer\n        for i in range(self.num_layers - 1):\n            layer = self.num_layers - i - 2\n\n            u_sampled, support, p_u = self.one_layer_sampling(adj=this_adj, adj_val=this_adj_val, x_v=this_x_v,\n                                                              output_size=self.layer_sizes[layer])\n\n            this_x_v = tf.gather(self.x, u_sampled)\n            this_adj = tf.gather(self.adj, u_sampled)\n            this_adj_val = tf.gather(self.adj_val, u_sampled)\n\n            all_x_u[layer] = this_x_v\n            all_support[layer] = support\n            all_p_u[layer] = p_u\n\n        return all_x_u, all_support, all_p_u\n\n    def one_layer_sampling(self, adj, adj_val, x_v, output_size):\n        \"\"\"layer wise sampling \"\"\"\n\n        support, u = from_adjlist(adj, adj_val)\n        x_u = tf.gather(self.x, u)\n        h_v = tf.reduce_sum(tf.matmul(x_v, tf.expand_dims(self.w_s[:, 1], -1))) # Corresponds to part of reward for node v\n        h_u = tf.matmul(x_u, tf.expand_dims(self.w_s[:, 0], -1)) # Corresponds to part of reward for node u\n        attention = tf.reshape(1 / tf.cast(output_size, tf.float32) * (tf.nn.relu(h_v + h_u) + 1), [-1]) # Adapted attention values (alpha'_ij)\n        g_u = tf.reshape(tf.nn.relu(h_u) + 1, [-1]) # Additional weight/factor for reward\n\n        # Sampling probability proportional to (alpha'_ij / q_ij^2) * ||h_j||^2 (simplified to p1 below)\n        # Note: The formulation `tf.cast(tf.sqrt(tf.sparse_reduce_sum(tf.square(support), axis=0)), dtype=tf.float32)`\n        # appears to be a proxy or component for ||h_j||^2 (norm of node embeddings/features)\n        # combined with attention and g_u, forming the basis for the sampling probability.\n        p1 = tf.cast(tf.sqrt(tf.sparse_reduce_sum(tf.square(support), axis=0)), dtype=tf.float32) * attention * g_u\n        p1 = p1 / tf.reduce_sum(p1) # Normalize probabilities\n\n        # Sample neighbors based on p1 (multinomial sampling as in EXP3/MAB)\n        samples = tf.cast(tf.multinomial([tf.log(p1)], output_size), tf.int64)\n\n        u_sampled = tf.gather(u, samples[0])\n        p_u = tf.gather(p1, samples[0]) # Probabilities for sampled nodes\n        support = SparsePlus(support)\n        support_sampled = support.gather_columns(samples[0])\n\n        return u_sampled, support_sampled, p_u\n\nfrom layers import *\nfrom metrics import *\n\nflags = tf.app.flags\nFLAGS = flags.FLAGS\n\n\nclass Model(object):\n    def __init__(self, **kwargs):\n        allowed_kwargs = {'name', 'logging'}\n        for kwarg in kwargs.keys():\n            assert kwarg in allowed_kwargs, 'Invalid keyword argument: ' + kwarg\n        name = kwargs.get('name')\n        if not name:\n            name = self.__class__.__name__.lower()\n        self.name = name\n\n        logging = kwargs.get('logging', False)\n        self.logging = logging\n\n        self.vars = {}\n        self.placeholders = {}\n\n        self.layers = []\n        self.activations = []\n\n        self.inputs = None\n        self.outputs = None\n\n        self.loss = 0\n        self.reg_loss = 0\n        self.accuracy = 0\n        self.optimizer = None\n        self.opt_op = None\n\n    def _build(self):\n        raise NotImplementedError\n\n    def build(self):\n        \"\"\" Wrapper for _build() \"\"\"\n        with tf.variable_scope(self.name):\n            self._build()\n\n        # Build sequential layer model\n        self.activations.append(self.inputs)\n\n        for layer in self.layers:\n            hidden, reg_loss = layer(self.activations[-1])\n            self.activations.append(hidden)\n\n        self.reg_loss += reg_loss\n        self.outputs = self.activations[-1]\n\n        # Store model variables for easy access\n        variables = tf.get_collection(tf.GraphKeys.GLOBAL_VARIABLES, scope=self.name)\n        self.vars = {var.name: var for var in variables}\n\n        # Build metrics\n        self._loss()\n        self._accuracy()\n\n        self.opt_op = self.optimizer.minimize(self.loss)\n\n    def predict(self):\n        pass\n\n    def _loss(self):\n        raise NotImplementedError\n\n    def _accuracy(self):\n        raise NotImplementedError\n\n    def save(self, sess=None):\n        if not sess:\n            raise AttributeError(\"TensorFlow session not provided.\")\n        saver = tf.train.Saver(self.vars)\n        save_path = saver.save(sess, \"tmp/%s.ckpt\" % self.name)\n        print(\"Model saved in file: %s\" % save_path)\n\n    def load(self, sess=None):\n        if not sess:\n            raise AttributeError(\"TensorFlow session not provided.\")\n        saver = tf.train.Saver(self.vars)\n        save_path = \"tmp/%s.ckpt\" % self.name\n        saver.restore(sess, save_path)\n        print(\"Model restored from file: %s\" % save_path)\n\n\nclass GCNAdapt(Model):\n    def __init__(self, placeholders, input_dim, sample_train=False, **kwargs):\n        super(GCNAdapt, self).__init__(**kwargs)\n        self.features = placeholders['features_inputs']\n        self.inputs = self.features[0]\n        self.input_dim = input_dim\n        # self.input_dim = self.inputs.get_shape().as_list()[1]  # To be supported in future Tensorflow versions\n        self.output_dim = placeholders['labels'].get_shape().as_list()[1]\n        self.placeholders = placeholders\n        self.supports = placeholders['support'] # Sampled support matrices\n        self.probs = placeholders['prob'] # Sampling probabilities from SamplerAdapt\n        self.optimizer = tf.train.AdamOptimizer(learning_rate=FLAGS.learning_rate)\n        with tf.variable_scope(self.name, reuse=True):\n            self.sample_params =tf.get_variable('sample_weights', [input_dim, 2]) # Learned weights for sampling/attention\n\n        # Apply attention using sampled support and probabilities\n        self.support_21 = self._attention(self.supports[0], self.features[0], self.features[1], self.probs[0])\n        self.support_32 = self._attention(self.supports[1], self.features[1], self.features[2], self.probs[1])\n\n        self.build()\n\n    def _loss(self):\n        # Weight decay loss\n        for var in self.layers[0].vars.values():\n            self.loss += FLAGS.weight_decay * tf.nn.l2_loss(var)\n\n        # Cross entropy error\n        self.loss += softmax_cross_entropy(self.outputs, self.placeholders['labels'])\n\n        # Variance reduction term (FLAGS.var is the coefficient for this regularization)\n        self.loss += FLAGS.var*self.reg_loss\n\n    def _accuracy(self):\n        self.accuracy = accuracy(self.outputs, self.placeholders['labels'])\n\n    def _attention(self, support, x_u, x_v, prob):\n        sample_params = self.sample_params\n        n_v = tf.shape(support)[0]\n        n_u = tf.shape(support)[1]\n        h_v = tf.matmul(x_v, tf.expand_dims(sample_params[:,0], -1))          # v*1\n        h_u = tf.matmul(x_u, tf.expand_dims(sample_params[:,1], -1))          # u*1\n        # Computes 'adjusted feedback attention values' (alpha'_ij) based on learned parameters\n        attention = 1/tf.cast(n_u, tf.float32) * (tf.nn.relu(h_v + tf.reshape(h_u, (1, n_u))) + 1)       # v*u\n        # Adjusts support matrix using the attention and sampling probabilities (alpha'_ij / q_ij)\n        support = support*(attention/tf.reshape(prob, (1, n_u))) \n\n        return support\n\n    def _build(self):\n        # First layer of graph convolution with adapted attention\n        self.layers.append(GraphSampleConvolution(input_dim=self.input_dim,\n                                                  output_dim=FLAGS.hidden1,\n                                                  placeholders=self.placeholders,\n                                                  support=self.support_21,\n                                                  act=tf.nn.relu,\n                                                  dropout=True,\n                                                  sparse_inputs=False,\n                                                  logging=self.logging))\n\n        # Second layer of graph convolution with adapted attention and variance regularization\n        self.layers.append(GraphSampleConvolutionReg(input_dim=FLAGS.hidden1,\n                                                      output_dim=self.output_dim,\n                                                      placeholders=self.placeholders,\n                                                      support=self.support_32,\n                                                      act=lambda x: x,\n                                                      dropout=True,\n                                                      logging=self.logging))\n\n    def predict(self):\n        return tf.nn.softmax(self.outputs)\n\nfrom inits import *\nimport tensorflow as tf\n\nflags = tf.app.flags\nFLAGS = flags.FLAGS\n\n_LAYER_UIDS = {}\n\ndef get_layer_uid(layer_name=''):\n    \"\"\"Helper function, assigns unique layer IDs.\"\"\"\n    if layer_name not in _LAYER_UIDS:\n        _LAYER_UIDS[layer_name] = 1\n        return 1\n    else:\n        _LAYER_UIDS[layer_name] += 1\n        return _LAYER_UIDS[layer_name]\n\n\ndef sparse_dropout(x, keep_prob, noise_shape):\n    \"\"\"Dropout for sparse tensors.\"\"\"\n    random_tensor = keep_prob\n    random_tensor += tf.random_uniform(noise_shape)\n    dropout_mask = tf.cast(tf.floor(random_tensor), dtype=tf.bool)\n    pre_out = tf.sparse_retain(x, dropout_mask)\n    return pre_out * (1./keep_prob)\n\n\ndef dot(x, y, sparse=False):\n    \"\"\"Wrapper for tf.matmul (sparse vs dense).\"\"\"\n    if sparse:\n        res = tf.sparse_tensor_dense_matmul(x, y)\n    else:\n        res = tf.matmul(x, y)\n    return res\n\n\nclass Layer(object):\n    \"\"\"Base layer class. Defines basic API for all layer objects.\n    Implementation inspired by keras (http://keras.io).\n\n    # Properties\n        name: String, defines the variable scope of the layer.\n        logging: Boolean, switches Tensorflow histogram logging on/off\n\n    # Methods\n        _call(inputs): Defines computation graph of layer\n            (i.e. takes input, returns output)\n        __call__(inputs): Wrapper for _call()\n        _log_vars(): Log all variables\n    \"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(self, **kwargs):\n        allowed_kwargs = {'name', 'logging'}\n        for kwarg in kwargs.keys():\n            assert kwarg in allowed_kwargs, 'Invalid keyword argument: ' + kwarg\n        name = kwargs.get('name')\n        if not name:\n            layer = self.__class__.__name__.lower()\n            name = layer + '_' + str(get_layer_uid(layer))\n        self.name = name\n        self.vars = {}\n        logging = kwargs.get('logging', False)\n        self.logging = logging\n        self.sparse_inputs = False\n\n    def _call(self, inputs):\n        return inputs\n\n    def __call__(self, inputs):\n        with tf.name_scope(self.name):\n            if self.logging and not self.sparse_inputs:\n                tf.summary.histogram(self.name + '/inputs', inputs)\n            outputs = self._call(inputs)\n            if self.logging:\n                for output in outputs:\n                    tf.summary.histogram(self.name + '/outputs', output)\n            return outputs\n\n    def _log_vars(self):\n        for var in self.vars:\n            tf.summary.histogram(self.name + '/vars/' + var, self.vars[var])\n\n\nclass GraphSampleConvolutionReg(Layer):\n    \"\"\"Graph convolution layer with regularization for variance reduction.\"\"\"\n    def __init__(self, input_dim, output_dim, placeholders,\n                 support,\n                 name = 'con_reg',\n                 sparse_support=True,\n                 sparse_inputs=False, act=tf.nn.relu, bias=False,\n                 dropout=False, featureless=False, **kwargs):\n        super(GraphSampleConvolutionReg, self).__init__(**kwargs)\n\n        if dropout:\n            self.dropout = placeholders['dropout']\n        else:\n            self.dropout = 0.\n\n        self.name = name\n        self.act = act\n        self.support = support\n        self.sparse_support = sparse_support\n        self.sparse_inputs = sparse_inputs\n        self.featureless = featureless\n        self.bias = bias\n        self.output_dim = output_dim\n        # helper variable for sparse dropout\n        self.num_features_nonzero = placeholders['num_features_nonzero']\n\n        with tf.variable_scope(self.name + '_vars'):\n            self.vars['weights'] = glorot([input_dim, output_dim],\n                                          name='weights')\n            if self.bias:\n                self.vars['bias'] = zeros([output_dim], name='bias')\n\n        if self.logging:\n            self._log_vars()\n\n    def _call(self, inputs):\n        x = inputs\n\n        # dropout\n        if self.sparse_inputs:\n            x = sparse_dropout(x, 1-self.dropout, self.num_features_nonzero)\n        else:\n            x = tf.nn.dropout(x, 1-self.dropout)\n\n        if not self.featureless:\n            pre_sup = dot(x, self.vars['weights'], sparse=self.sparse_inputs)\n        else:\n            pre_sup = self.vars['weights']\n\n        output = dot(self.support, pre_sup, sparse=self.sparse_support)\n\n        n_v = tf.cast(tf.shape(self.support)[0], tf.float32)\n        n_u = tf.cast(tf.shape(self.support)[1], tf.float32)\n\n        mean_output = tf.reshape(tf.reduce_mean(output, axis=0), (1, -1))\n        if self.sparse_support:\n            mean_support = 1.0/tf.cast(n_v, tf.float32)*tf.sparse_reduce_sum(self.support, axis=0)\n        else:\n            mean_support = 1.0/tf.cast(n_v, tf.float32)*tf.reduce_sum(self.support, axis=0)\n        diff = tf.reshape(mean_support, (-1,1))*pre_sup - mean_output\n        # Computes the variance reduction regularization term\n        reg = 1.0 / tf.cast(n_u * self.output_dim, tf.float32) * tf.reduce_sum(diff * diff)\n\n\n        # bias\n        if self.bias:\n            output += self.vars['bias']\n\n        return self.act(output), reg\n\nfrom __future__ import division\nfrom __future__ import print_function\n\nimport tensorflow as tf\n\nimport time\nimport os\nfrom inits import *\nfrom sampler import *\nfrom models import GCNAdapt, GCNAdaptMix\n\n# Set random seed\nseed = 123\nnp.random.seed(seed)\ntf.set_random_seed(seed)\n\n# Settings (relevant flags for adaptive sampling and variance reduction)\nflags = tf.app.flags\nFLAGS = flags.FLAGS\nflags.DEFINE_string('dataset', 'cora', 'Dataset string.')  # 'cora', 'citeseer', 'pubmed'\nflags.DEFINE_string('model', 'gcn_adapt', 'Model string.')  # 'gcn', 'gcn_appr'\nflags.DEFINE_float('learning_rate', 0.001, 'Initial learning rate.')\nflags.DEFINE_integer('epochs', 300, 'Number of epochs to train.')\nflags.DEFINE_integer('hidden1', 16, 'Number of units in hidden layer 1.')\nflags.DEFINE_float('dropout', 0.0, 'Dropout rate (1 - keep probability).')\nflags.DEFINE_float('weight_decay', 5e-4, 'Weight for L2 loss on embedding    # Prepare data\n    adj, adj_train, adj_val_train, features, train_features, y_train, y_test, test_index = prepare_pubmed(FLAGS.dataset, FLAGS.max_degree)\n\n    input_dim = features.shape[1]\n     "Experiment Result": "The method uses adaptive sampling and variance reduction. Two specific models are implemented: `GCNAdapt` and `GCNAdaptMix`. The sampling mechanism is handled by `SamplerAdapt`.\n\n### General Settings:\n- **Dataset**: `FLAGS.dataset` can be 'cora', 'citeseer', or 'pubmed'. The `run_pubmed.py` script specifically prepares data for 'pubmed' (or 'reddit').\n- **Model**: `FLAGS.model` is set to either 'gcn_adapt' or 'gcn_adapt_mix', which are the proposed models.\n- **Learning Rate**: `FLAGS.learning_rate` = 0.001 (for AdamOptimizer).\n- **Epochs**: `FLAGS.epochs` = 300.\n- **Hidden Layer Units**: `FLAGS.hidden1` = 16 (for the first hidden layer).\n- **Dropout Rate**: `FLAGS.dropout` = 0.0 (keep probability is 1 - dropout).\n- **Weight Decay**: `FLAGS.weight_decay` = 5e-4 (L2 loss on embedding matrix).\n- **Early Stopping Tolerance**: `FLAGS.early_stopping` = 30 epochs.\n- **Maximum Degree**: `FLAGS.max_degree` = 32 (for constructing the adjacent matrix, used in `compute_adjlist`).\n- **Sampler Device**: `FLAGS.sampler_device` = 'cpu' or 'gpu'.\n- **Nodes per Layer (`rank`)**: `FLAGS.rank` = 128. This directly controls the number of sampled nodes (`output_size`) for each layer in `SamplerAdapt`.\n- **Variance Reduction Weight**: `FLAGS.var` = 0.5. This factor scales the `reg_loss` term in the overall loss function of `GCNAdapt` (`self.loss += FLAGS.var*self.reg_loss`).\n\n### Model Specific Settings:\n- **GCNAdapt**: Uses 2 supports (sampled layers) and `layer_sizes = [FLAGS.rank, FLAGS.rank, 256]`. The `_attention` function in `GCNAdapt` uses learned `sample_params` (`w_s`) to adjust the support matrix based on feature embeddings and sampling probabilities, effectively implementing the 'adjusted feedback attention values' (alpha'_ij) divided by `q_ij` mechanism.\n- **GCNAdaptMix**: Uses 1 support and `layer_sizes = [FLAGS.rank, 256]` (only one adaptive sampling layer).\n\n### Sampling Process:\n- The `SamplerAdapt` class implements layer-wise sampling.\n- In `one_layer_sampling`, `self.w_s` (learned `sample_weights`) and node features (`x_v`, `x_u`) are used to calculate `attention` and `g_u` terms.\n- Sampling probabilities `p1` are derived proportionally to `sqrt(sparse_reduce_sum(square(support), axis=0)) * attention * g_u`. This formulation aims to approximate the reward related to variance reduction. The `tf.multinomial` function is used to draw samples based on these probabilities.\n\n### Variance Optimization:\n- The `GraphSampleConvolutionReg` layer computes a regularization term `reg` based on the difference between the weighted sum of `pre_sup` (feature transformation) and the mean output.\n- This `reg` term is added to the overall loss in `GCNAdapt` with a coefficient `FLAGS.var`, directly contributing to the sampling variance optimization objective outlined in the method."
