\"\"\"\n    Normalize adj according to the method of rw normalization.\n    Note that sym norm is used in the original GCN paper (kipf),\n    while rw norm is used in GraphSAGE and some other variants.\n    Here we don't perform sym norm since it doesn't seem to\n    help with accuracy improvement.\n\n    # Procedure:\n    #       1. adj add self-connection --> adj'\n    #       2. D' deg matrix from adj'\n    #       3. norm by D^{-1} x adj'\n    if sort_indices is True, we re-sort the indices of the returned adj\n    Note that after 'dot' the indices of a node would be in descending order\n    rather than ascending order\n    \"\"\"\n    diag_shape = (adj.shape[0],adj.shape[1])\n    D = adj.sum(1).flatten() if deg is None else deg\n    norm_diag = sp.dia_matrix((1/D,0),shape=diag_shape)\n    adj_norm = norm_diag.dot(adj)\n    if sort_indices:\n        adj_norm.sort_indices()\n    return adj_norm\n\n\n\n\n##################\n# PRINTING UTILS #\n#----------------#\n\n_bcolors = {'header': '\\033[95m',\n            'blue': '\\033[94m',\n            'green': '\\033[92m',\n            'yellow': '\\033[93m',\n            'red': '\\033[91m',\n            'bold': '\\033[1m',\n            'underline': '\\033[4m'}\n\n\ndef printf(msg,style=''):\n    if not style or style == 'black':\n        print(msg)\n    else:\n        print(\"{color1}{msg}{color2}\".format(color1=_bcolors[style],msg=msg,color2='\\033[0m'))"
}{
    "Title": "Hierarchical Graph Transformer with Adaptive Node Sampling",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper identifies two main deficiencies of current graph transformers: (1) existing node sampling strategies are agnostic to graph characteristics and training, often sampling uninformative nodes; and (2) most sampling strategies focus on local neighbors, neglecting long-range dependencies. To address these, the authors propose Hierarchical Graph Transformer with Adaptive Node Sampling (ANS-GT). Its main contributions are: (1) an Adaptive Node Sampling (ANS) module that formulates node sampling optimization as an adversary bandit problem to adaptively select informative nodes based on attention weights; and (2) a hierarchical attention scheme with graph coarsening to capture both fine-grained local and coarse-grained global information, reducing computational complexity while preserving long-range dependencies. The method achieves state-of-the-art results on several benchmark datasets and has linear computational complexity.",
    "Methodology": "The proposed ANS-GT model consists of two core components: 1. Adaptive Node Sampling (ANS): This module adaptively assigns weights to multiple sampling heuristics (1-hop neighbors, 2-hop neighbors, Personalized PageRank (PPR), and KNN based on cosine similarity of attributes) by formulating the optimization as an adversary bandit problem, modifying the Exp4.P method. Rewards are calculated based on the attention weights and the sampling probabilities, aiming to align sampling distributions with node significance. Weights are updated periodically during training. 2. Hierarchical Graph Attention: This scheme combines fine-grained local attention (using the adaptive node sampling) and coarse-grained global attention. For global attention, graph coarsening algorithms (e.g., Variational Neighborhoods) are applied to generate a smaller coarse graph, and sampled super-nodes from this graph, along with learnable global nodes, are incorporated into the attention mechanism to capture long-range dependencies efficiently. Positional encoding uses proximity encoding. The model uses a standard Transformer architecture with multi-head self-attention and feed-forward networks, optimized with AdamW and cross-entropy loss with bagging aggregation during inference.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The effectiveness of ANS-GT was evaluated on node classification tasks using six benchmark datasets: Cora, CiteSeer, PubMed (citation graphs); Chameleon, Squirrel (Wikipedia graphs); Actor (co-occurrence graph); and WebKB datasets (Cornell, Texas, Wisconsin). An additional evaluation was performed on OGB datasets (ogbn-arxiv, ogbn-products). The datasets were split into 60%/20%/20% for training, validation, and testing, respectively. Performance was measured using node classification accuracy. Baselines included 8 popular Graph Neural Networks (GCN, GAT, GraphSAGE, JKNet, APPNP, Geom-GCN, H2GCN, GPRGNN) and 4 state-of-the-art Graph Transformers (GT, SAN, Graphormer, Gophormer). The model was trained using AdamW optimizer with a peak learning rate of 2e-4, a 100-epoch warm-up, and linear decay. A single NVIDIA Tesla V100 GPU was used. Hyper-parameters (e.g., dropout rate 0.5, hidden dimension 128, 1,000 training epochs, sampling update period 100, 20 sampled nodes, 10 for proximity encoding, 8 attention heads) were tuned via grid search on each dataset, including batch size, number of data augmentations, number of layers, number of sampled nodes, super-nodes, and global nodes. Graph coarsening used Variational Neighborhoods with a default rate of 0.01.",
    "Limitations": "One limitation of the proposed work is the introduction of more hyper-parameters, which may require extensive fine-tuning. Additionally, the adaptive node sampling module, while beneficial, potentially introduces biases in sampling nodes for training.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The authors suggest exploring other positional encoding methods, such as Laplacian eigenvectors, in future work. More broadly, they hope their work can help generalize the Transformer architecture to the graph domain and encourage unified modeling of multi-modal data.",
    "Experiment Code": "def get_reward(args, model, device, loader, p):\n    column_normalized_adj = sp.load_npz('./dataset/' + args.dataset_name + '/column_normalized_adj.npz')\n    normalized_adj = sp.load_npz('./dataset/'+args.dataset_name+'/normalized_adj.npz')\n    data_x = torch.load('./dataset/' + args.dataset_name + '/x.pt')\n    normalized_adj1 = normalized_adj*normalized_adj\n    eigen_adj = 0.15 * inv((sp.eye(normalized_adj.shape[0]) - (1 - 0.15) * column_normalized_adj).toarray()) # PPR\n    eigen_adj1 = normalized_adj.toarray() # 1-hop\n    eigen_adj2 = normalized_adj1.toarray() # 2-hop\n    x = normalize(data_x, dim=1)\n    eigen_adj3 = np.array(torch.matmul(x, x.transpose(1, 0))) # KNN-attr\n    r = [[], [], [], []]\n    reward = np.zeros(4)\n    model.eval()\n    n_node = 10\n    for step, batch in enumerate(tqdm(loader, desc=\"Iteration\")):\n        batch = batch.to(device)\n        with torch.no_grad():\n            scores = model(batch, get_score=True)\n        scores = scores[:, 1:n_node]\n        for i, score in enumerate(torch.split(scores, args.num_data_augment, dim=0)):\n            ids = batch.ids[i*args.num_data_augment:(i+1)*args.num_data_augment]\n            id = ids[0, 0].cpu().item()\n            ids = ids[:, 1:n_node]\n            s = eigen_adj[id]\n            s1 = eigen_adj1[id]\n            s2 = eigen_adj2[id]\n            s3 = eigen_adj3[id]\n            s[id], s1[id], s2[id], s3[id] = 0, 0, 0, 0\n            s = torch.tensor(np.maximum(s, 0)).to(device)\n            s = s/(s.sum()+1e-5)\n            s1 = torch.tensor(np.maximum(s1, 0)).to(device)\n            s1 = s1/(s1.sum()+1e-5)\n            s2 = torch.tensor(np.maximum(s2, 0)).to(device)\n            s2 = s2/(s2.sum()+1e-5)\n            s3 = torch.tensor(np.maximum(s3, 0)).to(device)\n            s3 = s3 / (s3.sum() + 1e-5)\n            phi = p[0]*s + p[1]*s1 + p[2]*s2 + p[3]*s3 + 1e-5\n            r[0].append(torch.sum(score * s[ids] / phi[ids]) / args.num_data_augment)\n            r[1].append(torch.sum(score * s1[ids] / phi[ids]) / args.num_data_augment)\n            r[2].append(torch.sum(score * s2[ids] / phi[ids]) / args.num_data_augment)\n            r[3].append(torch.sum(score * s3[ids] / phi[ids]) / args.num_data_augment)\n    reward[0] = torch.mean(torch.cat([i.unsqueeze(0) for i in r[0]])).cpu().numpy()\n    reward[1] = torch.mean(torch.cat([i.unsqueeze(0) for i in r[1]])).cpu().numpy()\n    reward[2] = torch.mean(torch.cat([i.unsqueeze(0) for i in r[2]])).cpu().numpy()\n    reward[3] = torch.mean(torch.cat([i.unsqueeze(0) for i in r[3]])).cpu().numpy()\n    return reward\n\n# From main_adaptive.py (within main function's training loop for ANS weight update)\n# Initial sampling weights\nsampling_weight = np.ones(4)\np_min = 0.05\np = (1 - 4 * p_min) * sampling_weight / sum(sampling_weight) + p_min\n# Adaptive weight update (Exp4.P modification)\n# if epoch % args.weight_update_period == 0:\n#    r = get_reward(args, model, device, val_loader, p)\n#    sampling_weight = node_sampling function (core for ANS and Hierarchical Sampling)\ndef node_sampling(p=None):\n    # ... (loading data, adjacency matrices, node features, etc.) ...\n    # Heuristics definition\n    eigen_adj = c * inv((sp.eye(adj.shape[0]) - (1 - c) * column_normalized_adj).toarray())\n    eigen_adj1 = power_adj_list[0].toarray()\n    eigen_adj2 = power_adj_list[1].toarray()\n    x = normalize(data_x, dim=1)\n    eigen_adj3 = np.array(torch.matmul(x, x.transpose(1, 0)))\n\n    G = > 0)[0].item()\n    Coarse_adj = coarse_graph_adj(adj, C)\n    Coarse_graph_dim = Coarse_adj.shape[0]\n    normalized_coarse_graph = coarse_adj_normalize(Coarse_adj)\n    coarse_power_adj_list = [normalized_coarse_graph]\n    for m in range(5):\n        coarse_power_adj_list.append(np.matmul(normalized_coarse_graph, coarse_power_adj_list[m]))\n\n    data_list = []\n    for id in       super_node_id = node_supernode_dict[id]\n\n        s = eigen_adj[id]\n        s[id] = 0\n        s = np.maximum(s, 0)\n        if p is not None: # Apply adaptive weights for local node 0).sum())\n        sample_num2 = np.minimum(k2, (Coarse_adj[super_node_id] > 0).sum()) # Super-node sampling\n        for _ in range(Samples):\n            if sample_num1 > 0:\n                sample_index1 = np.random.choice(a=np.arange(data_y.shape[0]), size=sample_num1, replace=False, p=s/s.sum())\n            else:\n                sample_index1 = np.array([], dtype=int)\n\n            if sample_num2 > 0:\n                sample_index2 = np.random.choice(a=np.arange(Coarse_graph_dim), size=sample_num2, replace=False,\n in power_adj_list])\n\n            attn_bias_complem1 = torch.cat([torch.tensor(i[super_node_list, :][:, node2supernode_list], dtype=torch.float32).unsqueeze(0) for i in coarse_power_adj_list])\n            attn_bias_complem2 = torch.cat([torch.tensor(i[all_node_list, :][:, super_node_list], dtype=torch.float32).unsqueeze(0) for i in coarse_power_adj_list])\n\n            attn_bias = torch.cat([attn_bias, attn_bias_complem1], dim=1)\n            attn_bias = torch.cat([attn_bias, attn_bias_complem2], dim=2)\n\n            attn_bias = attn_bias.permute(1, 2, 0)\n\n            label = torch.cat([data_y[node_feature_id], torch.zeros(len(super_node_list))]).long()\n            feature_id = torch.cat([node_feature_id, torch.tensor(super_node_list + data_y.shape[0], dtype=int)])\n            sub_data_list.append([attn_bias, feature_id, label])\n        data_list.append(sub_data_list)\n    return data_list, feature\n\n# From graph_coarsening/coarsening_utils.py (utility for graph coarsening)\ndef coarsen(G, K=10, r=0.5, max_levels=20, method='variation_edges', algorithm='greedy', Uk=None, lk=None, max_level_r=0.99):\n    # ... (function body for various coarsening methods, including 'variation_neighborhood' via 'variation_linear') ...\n    # This function returns coarsening matrix C and coarse graph Gc\n\n# From model.py (Multi-Head Attention and Graph Transformer Model)\nclass MultiHeadAttention(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, hidden_size, attention_dropout_rate, num_heads, attn_bias_dim):\n        super(MultiHeadAttention, self).__init__()\n        self.num_heads = num_heads\n        self.att_size = att_size = hidden_size // num_heads\n        self.scale = att_size ** -0.5\n        self.linear_q = nn.Linear(hidden_size, num_heads * att_size)\n        self.linear_k = nn.Linear(hidden_size, num_heads * att_size)\n        self.linear_v = nn.Linear(hidden_size, num_heads * att_size)\n        self.linear_bias = nn.Linear(attn_bias_dim, num_heads) # Proximity encoding processing\n        self.att_dropout = nn.Dropout(attention_dropout_rate)\n        self.output_layer = nn.Linear(num_heads * att_size, hidden_size)\n\n    def forward(self, q, k, v, attn_bias=None, get_score=False):\n        # ... (standard multi-head attention computations) ...\n        attn_bias = self.linear_bias(attn_bias).permute(0, 3, 1, 2) # Apply linear projection to bias\n        # ...\n        x = torch.matmul(q, k)\n        if attn_bias is not None:\n            x = x + attn_bias # Add proximity bias to attention logits\n        x = torch.softmax(x, dim=3)\n        if get_score:\n            score = x[:, :, 0, :] * torch.norm(v, dim=3) # Extract attention scores for reward\n        x = self.att_dropout(x)\n        x = x.matmul(v)\n        # ...\n        if get_score:\n            return x, score.mean(dim=1)\n        else:\n            return x\n\nclass GT(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(\n        self, n_layers, num_heads, input_dim, hidden_dim, output_dim, attn_bias_dim,\n        dropout_rate, intput_dropout_rate, ffn_dim, num_global_node, attention_dropout_rate,\n    ):\n        super().__init__()\n        self.num_heads = num_heads\n        self.node_encoder = nn.Linear(input_dim, hidden_dim)\n        self.input_dropout = nn.Dropout(intput_dropout_rate)\n        encoders = [EncoderLayer(hidden_dim, ffn_dim, dropout_rate, attention_dropout_rate, num_heads, attn_bias_dim)\n                    for _ in range(n_layers)]\n        self.layers = nn.ModuleList(encoders)\n        self.n_layers = n_layers\n        self.final_ln = nn.LayerNorm(hidden_dim)\n        self.downstream_out_proj = nn.Linear(hidden_dim, output_dim)\n        self.hidden_dim = hidden_dim\n        self.num_global_node = num_global_node\n        self.graph_token = nn.Embedding(self.num_global_node, hidden_dim) # Learnable global node(s)\n        self.graph_token_virtual_distance = nn.Embedding(self.num_global_node, attn_bias_dim) # Positional encoding for global nodes\n        self.apply(lambda module: init_params(module, n_layers=n_layers))\n\n    def forward(self, batched_data, perturb=None, get_score=False):\n        attn_bias, x = batched_data.attn_bias, batched_data.x\n        n_graph, n_node = x.size()[:2]\n        graph_attn_bias = attn_bias.clone()\n        node_feature = self.node_encoder(x)\n        if perturb is not None:\n            node_feature += perturb\n\n        global_node_feature = self.graph_token.weight.unsqueeze(0).repeat(n_graph, 1, 1)\n        node_feature = torch.cat([node_feature, global_node_feature], dim=1) # Concatenate global node(s)\n\n        # Extend attention bias with global node virtual distances\n        graph_attn_bias = torch.cat([graph_attn_bias, self.graph_token_virtual_distance.weight.unsqueeze(0).unsqueeze(2).\n                                     repeat(n_graph, 1, n_node, 1)], dim=1)\n        graph_attn_bias = torch.cat(\n            [graph_attn_bias, self.graph_token_virtual_distance.weight.unsqueeze(0).unsqueeze(0).\n            repeat(n_graph, n_node+self.num_global_node, 1, 1)], dim=2)\n\n        output = self.input_dropout(node_feature)\n        if get_score:\n            for i, enc_layer in enumerate(self.layers):\n                if i == self.n_layers-1:\n                    score = enc_layer(output, graph_attn_bias, get_score=True)\n                else:\n                    output = enc_layer(output, graph_attn_bias)\n            return score\n        for enc_layer in self.layers:\n            output = enc_layer(output, graph_attn_bias)\n        output = self.final_ln(output)\n        output = self.downstream_out_proj(output[:, 0, :])\n        return F.log_softmax(output, dim=1)\n\n# From main_adaptive.py (eval function for bagging aggregation during inference)\ndef eval(args, model, device, loader):\n    # ... (evaluation loop) ...\n    # Aggregate predictions from multiple data augmentations\n    pred_list = []\n    for i in torch.split(y_pred, args.num_data_augment, dim=0):\n        pred_list.append(i.bincount().argmax().unsqueeze(0)) # Majority vote (mode)\n    y_pred = torch.cat(pred_list)\n    y_true = y_true.view(-1, args.num_data_augment)[:, 0] # Take first true label for comparison\n    # ...\n",
    "Experiment Result": "Model Parameters:\n- Number of Transformer layers (`n_layers`): 4\n- Number of attention heads (`num_heads`): 8\n- Hidden dimension (`hidden_dim`): 128\n- Feed-forward network dimension (`ffn_dim`): 128\n- Attention bias dimension (`attn_bias_dim`): 6 (derived from 6 powers of normalized adjacency for proximity encoding)\n- Input dropout rate (`intput_dropout_rate`): 0.1\n- Dropout rate (`dropout_rate`): 0.3\n- Attention dropout rate (`attention_dropout_rate`): 0.5\n- Number of learnable global nodes (`num_global_node`): 1\n\nAdaptive Node Sampling (ANS) Settings:\n- Four sampling heuristics:\n    1. Personalized PageRank (PPR) or diffusion kernel (calculated as `0.15 * inv((I - (1 - 0.15) * D_col_inv * A).toarray())`).\n    2. 1-hop neighbors (normalized adjacency matrix).\n    3. 2-hop neighbors (squared normalized adjacency matrix).\n    4. KNN based on cosine similarity of attributes (`torch.matmul(x, x.transpose(1, 0))`).\n- Initial sampling weights for heuristics: Uniform (`np.ones(4)`).\n- Minimum probability (`p_min`) for each heuristic: 0.05.\n- Weight update rule: `sampling_weight = sampling_weight * np.exp(2.0 * (r + 0.01 / p))` (a modification of the Exp4.P bandit algorithm).\n- Weight update period: Every 10,000 epochs (`args.weight_update_period`).\n\nHierarchical Graph Attention Settings:\n- Graph coarsening algorithm: `variation_neighborhood` (from `graph_coarsening.coarsen` function).\n- Coarsening parameters: `K=10` (subspace size to preserve), `r=0.9` (90% reduction).\n- Local node sampling (`k1`): 14 nodes are sampled based on the adaptively weighted combination of heuristics.\n- Super-node sampling (`k2`): 0 additional super-nodes are sampled in the provided `node_sampling` function.\n- Positional encoding: Uses powers of normalized adjacency matrices to generate `attn_bias_dim=6` proximity features.\n- Global attention: Incorporates 1 learnable global node (`num_global_node=1`) and its associated virtual distances/proximity encoding.\n\nTraining Settings:\n- Dataset: 'cora' (default).\n- Data split: Random split with `frac_train=0.6`, `frac_valid=0.2`, `frac_test=0.2`.\n- Random seed: 42.\n- Number of data augmentations per node (`num_data_augment`): 8 (used for training samples and inference bagging).\n- Optimizer: `AdamW`.\n- Peak learning rate (`peak_lr`): 2e-4.\n- End learning rate (`end_lr`): 1e-9.\n- Weight decay (`weight_decay`): 0.01.\n- Learning rate scheduler: `PolynomialDecayLR` with `warmup_epochs=100`, `total_epochs=1000`, and `power=1.0`.\n- Loss function: Cross-entropy loss (`F.nll_loss`).\n- Batch size (`batch_size`): 32.\n- Feature perturbation (`perturb_feature`): False.\n\nInference Settings:\n- Aggregation during inference: Bagging is performed by taking the majority vote (`bincount().argmax()`) across `num_data_augment` predictions for each node."
}{
    "Title": "Hierarchical Graph Transformer with Adaptive Node Sampling",
    "Main Contributions": "This paper addresses two main deficiencies of existing Graph Transformers for large graphs: (1) static node sampling strategies that are agnostic to graph characteristics and training process, and (2) focus on local neighbors neglecting long-range dependencies. The key contributions are the proposal of Adaptive Node Sampling for Graph Transformer (ANS-GT) which formulates node sampling as an adversary bandit problem to adaptively select informative nodes, and a hierarchical attention scheme with graph coarsening to capture long-range dependencies while maintaining computational efficiency.",
    "Methodology": "The Adaptive Node Sampling (ANS-GT) module optimizes node sampling by treating it as an adversary bandit problem, modifying the Exp4.P method. It adaptively assigns weights to various sampling heuristics (1-hop, 2-hop neighbors, PPR, and KNN based on attribute similarity) and combines their probabilities. Rewards are calculated based on the attention weights and sampling probabilities, encouraging sampling strategies that yield more significant nodes. The Hierarchical Graph Attention scheme combines fine-grained local attention (using ANS-GT) and coarse-grained global attention. Global attention utilizes graph coarsening algorithms (e.g., Variational Neighborhoods) to group nodes into super-nodes, reducing complexity and capturing long-range dependencies. The model integrates global nodes with learnable features and employs proximity encoding for positional information. The overall computational complexity is designed to be linear with the number of nodes (O(n)).",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on six benchmark datasets: Cora, CiteSeer, PubMed (citation graphs), Chameleon, Squirrel (Wikipedia graphs), Actor (co-occurrence graph), and WebKB datasets (Cornell, Texas, Wisconsin). Additional evaluations were performed on OGB datasets (ogbn-arxiv, ogbn-products). A 60%/20%/20% train-validation-test split was used for the initial datasets, and official splits for OGB. Baselines included 8 popular GNN methods (GCN, GAT, GraphSAGE, JKNet, APPNP, Geom-GCN, H2GCN, GPRGNN) and 4 state-of-the-art Graph Transformers (GT, SAN, Graphormer, Gophormer). Node classification accuracy was the evaluation metric. Training used the AdamW optimizer with a linear decay learning rate scheduler for 1,000 epochs on an NVIDIA Tesla V100 GPU. Hyper-parameters (e.g., dropout, hidden dimension, attention heads, number of sampled nodes/super-nodes/global nodes, data augmentation) were tuned using grid search.",
    "Limitations": "One limitation of the proposed work is the introduction of more hyper-parameters that require careful fine-tuning. Additionally, the adaptive node sampling approach may potentially introduce biases in the selection of nodes for training.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include exploring alternative positional encoding methods, such as Laplacian eigenvectors, within the proposed framework. The authors also generally hope their work can contribute to the unified modeling of multi-modal data.",
    "Experiment Code": "import torch\nimport torch.nn as nn\nimport torch.nn.functional as F\nimport numpy as np\nimport random\nfrom tqdm import tqdm\nimport scipy.sparse as sp\nfrom numpy.linalg import inv\nfrom torch.nn.functional import normalize\nimport pygsp as gsp\nfrom pygsp import graphs\nfrom sortedcontainers import SortedList\n\ndef get_reward(args, model, device, loader, p):\n    column_normalized_adj = sp.load_npz('./dataset/' + args.dataset_name + '/column_normalized_adj.npz')\n    normalized_adj = sp.load_npz('./dataset/'+args.dataset_name+'/normalized_adj.npz')\n    data_x = torch.load('./dataset/' + args.dataset_name + '/x.pt')\n    normalized_adj1 = normalized_adj*normalized_adj\n    eigen_adj = 0.15 * inv((sp.eye(normalized_adj.shape[0]) - (1 - 0.15) * column_normalized_adj).toarray())\n    eigen_adj1 = normalized_adj.toarray()\n    eigen_adj2 = normalized_adj1.toarray()\n    x = normalize(data_x, dim=1)\n    eigen_adj3 = np.array(torch.matmul(x, x.transpose(1, 0)))\n    r = [[], [], [], []]\n    reward = np.zeros(4)\n    model.eval()\n    n_node = 10\n    for step, batch in enumerate(tqdm(loader, desc=\"Iteration\")):\n        batch = batch.to(device)\n        with torch.no_grad():\n            scores = model(batch, get_score=True)\n        scores = scores[:, 1:n_node]\n        for i, score in enumerate(torch.split(scores, args.num_data_augment, dim=0)):\n            ids = batch.ids[i*args.num_data_augment:(i+1)*args.num_data_augment]\n            id = ids[0, 0].cpu().item()\n            ids = ids[:, 1:n_node]\n            s = eigen_adj[id]\n            s1 = eigen_adj1[id]\n            s2 = eigen_adj2[id]\n            s3 = eigen_adj3[id]\n            s[id], s1[id], s2[id], s3[id] = 0, 0, 0, 0\n            s = torch.tensor(np.maximum(s, 0)).to(device)\n            s = s/(s.sum()+1e-5)\n            s1 = torch.tensor(np.maximum(s1, 0)).to(device)\n            s1 = s1/(s1.sum()+1e-5)\n            s2 = torch.tensor(np.maximum(s2, 0)).to(device)\n            s2 = s2/(s2.sum()+1e-5)\n            s3 = torch.tensor(np.maximum(s3, 0)).to(device)\n            s3 = s3 / (s3.sum() + 1e-5)\n            phi = p[0]*s + p[1]*s1 + p[2]*s2 + p[3]*s3 + 1e-5\n            r[0].append(torch.sum(score * s[ids] / phi[ids]) / args.num_data_augment)\n            r[1].append(torch.sum(score * s1[ids] / phi[ids]) / args.num_data_augment)\n            r[2].append(torch.sum(score * s2[ids] / phi[ids]) / args.num_data_augment)\n            r[3].append(torch.sum(score * s3[ids] / phi[ids]) / args.num_data_augment)\n    reward[0] = torch.mean(torch.cat([i.unsqueeze(0) for i in r[0]])).cpu().numpy()\n    reward[1] = torch.mean(torch.cat([i.unsqueeze(0) for i in r[1]])).cpu().numpy()\n    reward[2] = torch.mean(torch.cat([i.unsqueeze(0) for i in r[2]])).cpu().numpy()\n    reward[3] = torch.mean(torch.cat([i.unsqueeze(0) for i in r[3]])).cpu().numpy()\n    return reward\n\n# Snippet from main_adaptive.py's main function showing adaptive sampling loop\n# ... (context before the loop)\n#     val_acc_list, test_acc_list = [], []\n#     sampling_weight = np.ones(4)\n#     weight_history = []\n#     p_min = 0.05\n#     p = (1 - 4 * p_min) * sampling_weight / sum(sampling_weight) + p_min\n#\n#     for epoch in range(1, args.epochs+1):\n#         # ... (rest of main function)\n\ndef adj_normalize(mx):\n    mx = mx + sp.eye(mx.shape[0])\n    rowsum = np.array(mx.sum(1))\n    r_inv = np.power(rowsum, -0.5).flatten()\n    r_inv[np.isinf(r_inv)] = 0.\n    r_mat_inv = sp.diags(r_inv)\n    mx = r_mat_inv.dot(mx).dot(r_mat_inv)\n    return mx\n\ndef column_normalize(mx):\n    rowsum = np.array(mx.sum(1))\n    r_inv = np.power(rowsum, -1.0).flatten()\n    r_inv[np.isinf(r_inv)] = 0.\n    r_mat_inv = sp.diags(r_inv)\n    mx = mx.dot(r_mat_inv)\n    return mx\n\ndef coarse_graph_adj(mx, p):\n    p[p > 0] = 1.\n    p = np.array(p)\n    rowsum = p.sum(1)\n    r_inv = np.power(rowsum, -0.5).flatten()\n    r_inv[np.isinf(r_inv)] = 0.\n    r_mat_inv = np.diag(r_inv)\n    p = np.matmul(p.T, r_mat_inv)\n    mx = np.matmul(mx.toarray(), p)\n    mx = np.matmul(p.T, mx)\n    return mx\n\ndef coarse_adj_normalize(adj):\n    adj += np.diag(np.ones(adj.shape[0]))\n    r_inv = np.power(adj.sum(1), -0.5).flatten()\n    r_inv[np.isinf(r_inv)] = 0.\n    r_mat_inv = np.diag(r_inv)\n    adj = np.matmul(r_mat_inv, adj)\n    adj = np.matmul(adj, r_mat_inv)\n    return adj\n\ndef node_sampling(p=None):\n    name = 'cora'\n    edge_index = torch.load('./dataset/'+name+'/edge_index.pt')\n    data_x = torch.load('./dataset/'+name+'/x.pt')\n    data_y = torch.load('./dataset/'+name+'/y.pt')\n    adj = sp.coo_matrix((np.ones(edge_index.shape[1]), (edge_index[0], edge_index[1])),\n                                shape=(data_y.shape[0], data_y.shape[0]),\n                                dtype=np.float32)\n    normalized_adj = sp.load_npz('./dataset/'+name+'/normalized_adj.npz')\n    column_normalized_adj = sp.load_npz('./dataset/' + name + '/column_normalized_adj.npz')\n    c = 0.15\n    k1 = 14\n    k2 = 0\n    Samples = 8\n    power_adj_list = [normalized_adj]\n    for m in range(5):\n        power_adj_list.append(power_adj_list[0]*power_adj_list[m])\n\n    eigen_adj = c * inv((sp.eye(adj.shape[0]) - (1 - c) * column_normalized_adj).toarray())\n    eigen_adj1 = power_adj_list[0].toarray()\n    eigen_adj2 = power_adj_list[1].toarray()\n    x = normalize(data_x, dim=1)\n    eigen_adj3 = np.array(torch.matmul(x, x.transpose(1, 0)))\n\n    G = graphs.Graph(adj)\n    C, Gc, _, _ = coarsen(G, K=10, r=0.9, method='variation_neighborhood')\n    C = torch.tensor(C/C.sum(1), dtype=torch.float32)\n    super_node_feature = torch.matmul(C, data_x)\n    feature = torch.cat([data_x, super_node_feature])\n    node_supernode_dict = {}\n    for i in range(data_y.shape[0]):\n        node_supernode_dict[i] = torch.where(C[:, i] > 0)[0].item()\n    Coarse_adj = coarse_graph_adj(adj, C)\n    Coarse_graph_dim = Coarse_adj.shape[0]\n    normalized_coarse_graph = coarse_adj_normalize(Coarse_adj)\n    coarse_power_adj_list = [normalized_coarse_graph]\n    for m in range(5):\n        coarse_power_adj_list.append(np.matmul(normalized_coarse_graph, coarse_power_adj_list[m]))\n\n    data_list = []\n    for id in range(data_y.shape[0]):\n        sub_data_list = []\n        s = eigen_adj[id]\n        s[id] = -1000.0\n        top_neighbor_index = s.argsort()[-(k1+k2):]\n        super_node_id = node_supernode_dict[id]\n\n        s = eigen_adj[id]\n        s[id] = 0\n        s = np.maximum(s, 0)\n        if p is not None:\n            s1 = eigen_adj1[id]\n            s2 = eigen_adj2[id]\n            s3 = eigen_adj3[id]\n            s1[id] = 0\n            s2[id] = 0\n            s3[id] = 0\n            s1 = np.maximum(s1, 0)\n            s2 = np.maximum(s2, 0)\n            s3 = np.maximum(s3, 0)\n            s = p[0]*s/(s.sum()+1e-5) + p[1]*s1/(s1.sum()+1e-5) + p[2]*s2/(s2.sum()+1e-5) + p[3]*s3/(s3.sum()+1e-5)\n        sample_num1 = np.minimum(k1, (s > 0).sum())\n        sample_num2 = np.minimum(k2, (Coarse_adj[super_node_id] > 0).sum())\n        for _ in range(Samples):\n            if sample_num1 > 0:\n                sample_index1 = np.random.choice(a=np.arange(data_y.shape[0]), size=sample_num1, replace=False, p=s/s.sum())\n            else:\n                sample_index1 = np.array([], dtype=int)\n\n            if sample_num2 > 0:\n                sample_index2 = np.random.choice(a=np.arange(Coarse_graph_dim), size=sample_num2, replace=False,\n                                                 p=Coarse_adj[super_node_id]/Coarse_adj[super_node_id].sum())\n            else:\n                sample_index2 = np.array([], dtype=int)\n\n            node_feature_id = torch.cat([torch.tensor([id, ]), torch.tensor(sample_index1, dtype=int),\n                                    torch.tensor(top_neighbor_index[: k1 + k2 - sample_num2 - sample_num1],\n                                                      dtype=int)])\n\n            super_node_list = np.concatenate([[super_node_id], sample_index2])\n            node2supernode_list = np.array([node_supernode_dict[i.item()] for i in node_feature_id])\n            all_node_list = np.concatenate([node2supernode_list, super_node_list])\n\n            attn_bias = torch.cat(\n                [torch.tensor(i[node_feature_id, :][:, node_feature_id].toarray(), dtype=torch.float32).unsqueeze(0) for\n                 i in power_adj_list])\n\n            attn_bias_complem1 = torch.cat([torch.tensor(i[super_node_list, :][:, node2supernode_list], dtype=torch.float32).unsqueeze(0) for i in coarse_power_adj_list])\n            attn_bias_complem2 = torch.cat([torch.tensor(i[all_node_list, :][:, super_node_list], dtype=torch.float32).unsqueeze(0) for i in coarse_power_adj_list])\n\n            attn_bias = torch.cat([attn_bias, attn_bias_complem1], dim=1)\n            attn_bias = torch.cat([attn_bias, attn_bias_complem2], dim=2)\n\n            attn_bias = attn_bias.permute(1, 2, 0)\n\n            label = torch.cat([data_y[node_feature_id], torch.zeros(len(super_node_list))]).long()\n            feature_id = torch.cat([node_feature_id, torch.tensor(super_node_list + data_y.shape[0], dtype=int)])\n\n            sub_data_list.append([attn_bias, feature_id, label])\n        data_list.append(sub_data_list)\n\n    return data_list, feature\n\nclass MultiHeadAttention(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, hidden_size, attention_dropout_rate, num_heads, attn_bias_dim):\n        super(MultiHeadAttention, self).__init__()\n\n        self.num_heads = num_heads\n\n        self.att_size = att_size = hidden_size // num_heads\n        self.scale = att_size ** -0.5\n\n        self.linear_q = nn.Linear(hidden_size, num_heads * att_size)\n        self.linear_k = nn.Linear(hidden_size, num_heads * att_size)\n        self.linear_v = nn.Linear(hidden_size, num_heads * att_size)\n        self.linear_bias = nn.Linear(attn_bias_dim, num_heads)\n        self.att_dropout = nn.Dropout(attention_dropout_rate)\n\n        self.output_layer = nn.Linear(num_heads * att_size, hidden_size)\n\n    def forward(self, q, k, v, attn_bias=None, get_score=False):\n        orig_q_size = q.size()\n\n        d_k = self.att_size\n        d_v = self.att_size\n        batch_size = q.size(0)\n\n        q = self.linear_q(q).view(batch_size, -1, self.num_heads, d_k)\n        k = self.linear_k(k).view(batch_size, -1, self.num_heads, d_k)\n        v = self.linear_v(v).view(batch_size, -1, self.num_heads, d_v)\n        attn_bias = self.linear_bias(attn_bias).permute(0, 3, 1, 2)\n\n        q = q.transpose(1, 2)\n        v = v.transpose(1, 2)\n        k = k.transpose(1, 2).transpose(2, 3)\n\n        q = q * self.scale\n        x = torch.matmul(q, k)\n        if attn_bias is not None:\n            x = x + attn_bias\n\n        x = torch.softmax(x, dim=3)\n        if get_score:\n            score = x[:, :, 0, :] * torch.norm(v, dim=3)\n        x = self.att_dropout(x)\n        x = x.matmul(v)\n\n        x = x.transpose(1, 2).contiguous()\n        x = x.view(batch_size, -1, self.num_heads * d_v)\n\n        x = self.output_layer(x)\n\n        assert x.size() == orig_q_size\n        if get_score:\n            return x, score.mean(dim=1)\n        else:\n            return x\n\nclass EncoderLayer(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, hidden_size, ffn_size, dropout_rate, attention_dropout_rate, num_heads, attn_bias_dim):\n        super(EncoderLayer, self).__init__()\n\n        self.self_attention_norm = nn.LayerNorm(hidden_size)\n        self.self_attention = MultiHeadAttention(\n            hidden_size, attention_dropout_rate, num_heads, attn_bias_dim)\n        self.self_attention_dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout_rate)\n\n        self.ffn_norm = nn.LayerNorm(hidden_size)\n        self.ffn = FeedForwardNetwork(hidden_size, ffn_size, dropout_rate)\n        self.ffn_dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout_rate)\n\n\n    def forward(self, x, attn_bias=None, get_score=False):\n        y = self.self_attention_norm(x)\n        if get_score:\n            _, score = self.self_attention(y, y, y, attn_bias, get_score=True)\n            return score\n        y = self.self_attention(y, y, y, F.log_softmax(output, dim=1)\n\ndef coarsen(G, K=10, r=0.5, max_levels=20, method='variation_edges', algorithm='greedy', Uk=None, lk=None, max_level_r=0.99):\n    r = np.clip(r, 0, 0.999)\n    G0 =     A = B\n            else:\n                B = iC.dot(B)\n                d, V = np.linalg.eig(B.T @ (G.L).dot(B))\n                mask = (d==0); d[mask] = 1; dinvsqrt = d**(-1/2); dinvsqrt[mask] method, K=K)\n\n            if algorithm == 'optimal':\n                weights = -weights\n                if ('rss' not in method) : weights -= min(weights)\n                coarsening_list = matching_optimal(G, weights=weights, r=r_cur)\n\n            elif algorithm == 'greedy':\n                coarsening_list = matching_greedy(G, weights=weights, r=r_cur)\n\n        iC =        ones = np.ones(nc)\n        W = W_lil[nodes,:][:,nodes]\n        L = np.diag(2*deg[nodes] - W.dot(ones)) - W\n        B = (np.eye(nc) - np.outer(ones, ones) / nc ) @ A[nodes,:]\n        return np.linalg.norm(B.T @ L @ B)/(nc-1)\n\n    class CandidateSet:\n        def __init__(self, candidate_list):\n            self.set  = candidate_list\n            self.cost = subgraph_cost(candidate_list)\n        def __lt__(self, other):\n            return self.cost < other.cost\n\n    family = []\n    W_bool = G.A + sp.sparse.eye(G.N, dtype=np.bool_, format='csr')\n     "Experiment Result": "The Adaptive Node Sampling (ANS-GT) module optimizes node sampling by treating it as an adversary bandit problem, modifying the Exp4.P method. It adaptively assigns weights (p) to various sampling heuristics and combines their probabilities. Rewards are calculated based on attention weights and sampling probabilities, encouraging sampling strategies that yield more significant nodes. The four sampling heuristics used are: Personalized PageRank (PPR) with a teleport probability `c=0.15`, 1-hop neighbors, 2-hop neighbors, and KNN based on attribute similarity.The Hierarchical Graph Attention scheme combines fine-grained local attention (using ANS-GT) and coarse-grained global attention. Global attention utilizes the 'variation_neighborhood' graph coarsening algorithm from PyGSP to group nodes into super-nodes, reducing complexity and capturing long-range dependencies. The model integrates global nodes with learnable features (via `graph_token` embeddings) and employs proximity encoding for positional information (via `graph_token_virtual_distance` embeddings and `attn_bias_dim=6`).The experimental settings are as follows: The method is evaluated on the 'cora' dataset. The Graph Transformer model has `n_layers=4`, `num_heads=8`, `hidden_dim=128`, `ffn_dim=128`. Each node in the input graph is associated with `num_data_augment=8` sampled subgraphs. For sampling, `k1=14` local neighbors and `k2=0` global neighbors are considered. The model includes `num_global_node=1` global node. The training runs for `epochs=1000` with a `warmup_epochs=100` period. The learning rate (`peak_lr=2e-4`) decays to `end_lr=1e-9` with `weight_decay=0.01`. The `batch_size` is 32. The sampling weights for the heuristics are updated every `weight_update_period=10000` epochs based on the calculated rewards. Feature perturbation (`perturb_feature`) is set to `False`."

    "Title": "Layer-Neighbor Sampling --- Defusing Neighborhood Explosion in GNNs",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces LAyer-neighBOR sampling (LABOR), a novel algorithm for training Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) at scale, designed as a direct replacement for Neighbor Sampling (NS). LABOR effectively defuses the neighborhood explosion phenomenon by correlating sampling decisions among seed nodes, leading to up to 7 times fewer vertices and 4 times fewer edges sampled compared to NS, without sacrificing model quality. It ensures that the variance of each vertex's estimator matches NS, converges faster than existing layer sampling approaches under the same vertex sampling budget, and enables up to 112 times larger batch sizes. Additionally, the paper proposes Poisson Sampling for GNNs, applying it to the LADIES method to create PLADIES, which outperforms LADIES by up to 2% in F1-score and is more computationally efficient.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves combining the strengths of node-based (Neighbor Sampling) and layer-based sampling methods using a vertex-centric variance framework and Poisson Sampling. The proposed LABOR algorithm aims to ensure that the expected number of sampled neighbors for each seed vertex (E[˜ds]) matches a given fanout k, similar to Neighbor Sampling, while minimizing the total number of unique sampled vertices. This is achieved by defining a scalar `cs` for each seed node `s` that satisfies an equality relating the variance of the estimator to the target variance of Neighbor Sampling. Sampling for each vertex `t` in the neighborhood `N(S)` occurs with probability `cs*πt`, where `πt` are unnormalized probabilities. The algorithm computes `cs` and an optimal `π*` (minimizing the expected total number of unique sampled vertices) using fixed-point iterations. `cs` is found via a monotonic iterative algorithm, and `π` is updated iteratively to reduce the objective function value until convergence. Poisson sampling is unbiased by construction and performs well against sampling with replacement from a variance perspective. The formulation also considers an extension to nonuniform edge weights.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The methods were empirically evaluated in a node-prediction setting across four datasets: reddit, products, yelp, and flickr, which vary in size, density, and number of features. The GCN model was used with 3 layers, 256 hidden dimensions, and residual skip connections, optimized with Adam (0.001 learning rate). Experiments were implemented in DGL with a PyTorch backend and repeated 100 times, presenting averages. The study compared LABOR variants (LABOR-0, LABOR-1, LABOR-* corresponding to 0, 1, or converged fixed-point iterations), PLADIES, Neighbor Sampling (NS), and LADIES. Evaluation metrics included vertex/edge efficiency (total sampled vertices/edges), validation F1-score and training loss convergence curves (vs. cumulative sampled vertices/edges and training iterations), iterations per second, and test F1-score. Further experiments investigated batch size under a fixed vertex sampling budget and the convergence behavior of fixed-point iterations. Runtime performance was also evaluated for the GATv2 model, and hyperparameter optimization using HEBO was performed to compare wall-time to convergence against NS for specific validation accuracy targets on products and yelp datasets, using NVIDIA T4 and A100 GPUs.",
    "Limitations": "Existing layer sampling methods, like LADIES, suffer from several issues, including sub-optimal variance due to sampling with replacement (even if implemented without replacement, leading to bias), no guarantee of sufficient neighbors for each vertex (resulting in highly variable estimator quality), and difficulty in reasoning about and selecting layer-specific hyperparameters. Node-based sampling methods are noted to suffer most from the neighborhood explosion phenomenon by sampling too shallow subgraphs. While LABOR variants offer different trade-offs, LABOR-0 and LABOR-1 sample more vertices/edges than the fully optimized LABOR-* version, meaning the choice depends on the speed of feature access (slow storage favors LABOR-*). The current analysis simplifies variance by assuming Var(Mt) = 1 and uncorrelated |Mt| and πt. The paper's scope is limited to comparing against basic NS and LADIES, explicitly leaving the integration with methods using historical embeddings, magnitudes, or vertex caches for future work.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include investigating the integration of the proposed LABOR and PLADIES methods with other sampling algorithms that leverage additional information, such as historical embeddings, magnitudes of embeddings, or specialized sampling structures like vertex caches. Another key direction is to develop a more principled and systematic approach for choosing the variance target (`vs`) in the weighted graph setting. This involves exploring how to set `vs` as a function of the fanout parameter `k` by analyzing the derivative of the variance with respect to the expected number of sampled vertices, potentially leading to a formulation like `∂vs/∂E[˜ds] = C(k)`.",

{'open_problems': '1. Existing attention–aware samplers eliminate redundant neighbours but still oversample highly correlated vertices, which contributes little additional information and wastes memory bandwidth.\n2. The neighbour–importance signal (attentions, gradient norms) is re-estimated from scratch every iteration although it evolves smoothly during training, causing avoidable compute and variance.\n3. None of the current samplers provides any guarantee on inter-mini-batch diversity, so successive batches often revisit identical neighbourhoods – harmful for parallel data-parallel training where batches are processed simultaneously.\n4. GAT training energy footprint is rarely reported; there is no standard metric that jointly accounts for accuracy, wall-clock time and energy.\n5. Practical adoption is hindered by the need to hand-tune the per-layer fan-out k; an auto-tuning rule that respects a global memory / energy budget is missing.', 'methods': 'We propose DPA-HABLS (Diversity-Preserving, Adaptive Hierarchical Bandit Layer Sampling), a drop-in PyG / DGL neighbour-sampler that extends HABLS with three new ideas.\n\nM1 – Determinantal diversity filter (DPP).\nAfter each layer-candidate set C_ℓ is produced (≈3×k unique nodes) we build a cosine-similarity kernel K over their current embeddings (cached from previous forward pass).  We draw a k-subset S_ℓ via fast k-DPP sampling (Batched Greedy) instead of DepRound.  Expectation and inclusion probabilities are tracked so unbiasedness & ≤3·Var(NS) are preserved.  This provably minimises pair-wise redundancy while keeping the HABLS variance bound.\n\nM2 – Momentum attention cache.\nFor every edge (i,j) we maintain an exponential-moving-average  \\tilde α_{ij}  of the unnormalised attention across iterations.  The bandit reward becomes r_j = −|g_j|²·\\tilde α_{ij}.  The cache is updated cheaply without additional forward passes and lets the bandit converge faster; we prove that the bias introduced by stale attentions decays geometrically.\n\nM3 – Global budget controller.\nGiven a user-supplied GPU-memory or energy budget B we solve a tiny convex program each epoch that outputs per-layer fan-outs k_ℓ to maximise the predicted test-accuracy proxy A(k)=Σℓ w_ℓ·log k_ℓ subject to Σℓ c_ℓ·k_ℓ ≤ B, where c_ℓ is the sampled-vertex cost measured on-line.  The weights w_ℓ are estimated from the running variance reduction of each layer.  This removes manual tuning.\n\nM4 – Energy-aware score.\nWe add real-time power sampling (NVIDIA NVML) and report EATS = Energy·Area under Training-accuracy curve (↓ better).  This metric is logged for all baselines.\n\nTheory highlights: (i) k-DPP sampling with marginal constraints keeps unbiasedness; (ii) variance ≤ 3·Var(NS) still holds; (iii) the momentum cache adds ≤O(β^t) bias after t steps where β<1 is the decay factor.', 'experimental_setup': 'Datasets: reddit, flickr, ogbn-products, ogbn-arxiv; + new heterophilic datasets roman-empire, amazon-ratings to test diversity benefits.\nModels: 2-layer GAT, 4-layer GATv2, 3-layer GeniePath, 6-layer Spexphormer (graph transformer) to show generality.\nBaselines: NS(25), GraphSAINT-rw, LABOR-*, PLADIES, GNN-BS.M, original HABLS.\nMetrics: test acc / micro-F1, wall-clock to 95 % val-acc, peak GPU-mem, sampled vertices/edges, estimator variance, EATS (kWh·h), CO₂ estimate (via CodeCarbon).\nProtocol: 5 runs, fixed seeds, Adam (0.005), early-stop 50.  Budget controller evaluated at three budgets: Fast (≤6 GB), Balanced (≤10 GB), Accurate (≤14 GB).\nAblations: (a) w/o DPP, (b) w/o cache, (c) static k, (d) beta ∈ {0.0,0.5,0.9}.', 'experimental_code': 'import torch, torch.nn.functional as F\nfrom torch_geometric.loader import NeighborLoader\nfrom fast_dpp import sample_k_dpp  # pip install fast-dpp\n\nclass MomentumCache:\n    def __init__(self,beta=0.9):\n        self.beta=beta; self.store={}\n    def update(self,edges,alpha):\n        # edges: [E,2] tensor, alpha: [E]\n        for (i,j),a in zip(edges.tolist(),alpha.tolist()):\n            key=(i,j)\n            self.store[key]=self.beta*self.store.get(key,0.0)+(1-self.beta)*a\n    def get(self,js):\n        return torch.tensor([self.store.get(tuple(edge),0.0) for edge in js])\n\nclass DPABanditLayer:\n    def __init__(self,k,eta=0.3,beta=0.9):\n        self.k=k; self.eta=eta; self.w=None; self.cache=MomentumCache(beta)\n    @torch.no_grad()\n    def sample(self,cand_emb,cand_edges,grad,alpha_raw):\n        # cand_emb: [m,d]   cand_edges: [m,2]\n        if self.w is None:\n            self.w=torch.ones(cand_emb.size(0),device=cand_emb.device)\n        alpha=self.cache.get(cand_edges)\n        reward=(-grad.abs()*alpha).cpu()\n        p=(1-1e-3)*self.w/self.w.sum()+1e-3\n        sel_idx=sample_k_dpp(cand_emb.cpu().numpy(),self.k)  # diversity step\n        est=torch.zeros_like(p); est[sel_idx]=reward[sel_idx]/p[sel_idx]\n        self.w*=torch.exp(self.eta*est.to(self.w.device))\n        self.cache.update(cand_edges.cpu(),alpha_raw.cpu())\n        return sel_idx\n\n# integration with LABOR candidate producer identical to HABLS, replaced by DPABanditLayer', 'expected_result': '• DPA-HABLS reduces sampled vertices by 5–6× over NS, 2× over LABOR-*, 1.3× over HABLS.\n• Test accuracy improves by +1.0 % on ogbn-products and +1.8 % on heterophilic sets due to diversity.\n• Wall-clock speed-up: 40–55 % vs. LABOR-*, 25 % vs. HABLS.\n• EATS drops by 45 % relative to LABOR-* and 30 % to HABLS; The ideas generalise beyond GNNs to any sparse attention architecture and suggest a new research direction on diversity-controlled adaptive sampling under resource constraints.'}
